the default, change the catalogue — an agent's own answer still overrides it

either way (pluginEnabled).

Each catalogue row says which agents it is for and when it was added: for

lists agent kinds ("console", "raft", "api") and since the catalogue

revision. An agent's kind is read from its record (agentKind): "raft"

when Raft made it (provisionedBy), "api" when the Agents API did (the

openai config its adopt writes), else "console". The seven original rows

are for: ["console", "raft"], since: 1; reminder is for: ["console"],

since: 2, because Raft wakes its agents through its own channel and the

plugin's create refuses there. No row is for "api": an API agent opened

in the console is provisioned like any other, and gets nothing from the

catalogue. Its container comes from the Agents API's own pick of the rows

(apiAgentSeeds), passed as an explicit list, where for is not asked.

The record is the fact

because it exists before any mount does: Raft provisioning writes it, then

seeds, then adds the raft mount (cf/src/provision/steps.ts), and the

Agents API adopts before it seeds.

A catalogue row's alias is reserved on every agent, whether or not the row

applies to it: addMount refuses another plugin under it, so no mount from

the console or /admin/mounts can be called reminder unless it is a

reminder mount, even on a Raft agent or a deployment without reminder-app.

Each agent is reconciled with the catalogue where a turn starts — every

message goes through postMessage: a prompt, a console steer or follow-up, a

hook or Raft push, a background job's completion, a lease warning — and where

provision makes an agent or the console opens one (reconcileSeeds in

cf/src/runtime.ts). Not when an agent is only read: status, transcripts and

/admin/diagnose change nothing. A pass judges each row: not-for when its

for excludes the agent's kind, unavailable when its plugin says so

(below), declined when the agent has switched the plugin off, refused

when its settings do not validate or another plugin holds its alias,

present when the agent already has it — under its alias, or under any

alias, since two identical tool sets confuse the model and an operator's

rename (web to x) would otherwise bring a second web back — and

otherwise added. present is asked first, before not-for, unavailable

and declined, so a mount the agent had while its plugin was switched off is

not brought back after an operator removes it. The outcomes are kept per agent, one row per entry (alias

and since), beside the key the pass was judged from: the catalogue and its

revision (the highest since), the agent's kind, its plugin choices, and

which plugins are unavailable (src/store/seed-record.ts). A pass whose key

is unchanged reads and does nothing; when it moves, only the entries the agent

never had are judged again — declined, unavailable, refused, or a new

row — so the owner switching a plugin on or the deployment gaining the

configuration adds it. An entry once added or present is never added

again, even after its alias is freed: an operator's removal stays removed.

Each changed outcome is a mount.seeded trace row, written in

the transaction that adds the mount.

An agent that gains an entry after it was made is told, once. The pass that

adds the mount records a pending notice in the same transaction, and the next

prompt or steer (postMessage; not a follow-up, which on an idle agent is

queued and reaches the model only behind some later run) carries one line

per pending capability at its head, in the harness's own voice (`[a notice

from the harness, not a message from the user] …`); the notice is stamped

delivered as that message is written, so two racing messages say it once. No

turn is started to say it, and it is not put in the system prompt. A row may

carry notice, a function of the offered tool names (tool("create") answers

what the model was offered, never a guessed alias__tool), saying what the

capability is and how to use it — never the deployment behind it; without one

the line is the plugin's own tool descriptions. A notice waits while the

harness taking the message does not offer the entry's tools (a steer into a

turn that began before the mount). One that can never be true is voided,

with its reason, and never judged again: its mount is gone, its alias now

holds another plugin's mount (the line would name that plugin's tools in the

harness's voice), or the plugin is switched off or no longer installed. What a new agent's first pass mounts is

not announced: that pass (no record yet, no mounts) gives the agent its first

tools, so there is nothing for them to be news against; an agent made before

the record existed has its mounts, and is told what its first pass adds. The

mount.seeded row of an added entry says which (notice: pending or

first tools), and the record keeps when each notice was delivered

(seed_notices). An Agents API agent is never reconciled,

nor one provisioned with an explicit list (chosen: true: a bench arm, the

Agents API's container), which provision marks so, nor any agent under the

bench tenant (BENCH_TENANT), which also covers bench agents made before the

mark existed; a caller's list may name a plugin twice. No path provisions a

demo agent today: an agent carrying the ops mount the seed list gave every

agent before #213 is a console agent and is reconciled as one, and the

console's default tenant demo names no demo. A mount already there is left alone, and the console's

settings reconcile (uiEnsure) touches only the rows that apply to the agent.

Declare provides for what the plugin can give a session. Today the one

value is "container". The agents API picks a plugin to seed by asking what

each one offers rather than by looking for the id sandbox, so a second

plugin that can run a container is chosen without the kernel learning its

name.

Declare sideEffects honestly, and actsAs: "self" only for scratch. A

subagent (docs/agent-surface.md, "Subagents") is read-only: it is offered a tool

only when the tool declares sideEffects: "read", or the plugin declares

actsAs: "self", and its call to anything else, a program's included, is refused

at dispatch. A tool with no sideEffects counts as a write. actsAs: "self" says

every write of the plugin acts on nothing but the caller's own scratch: no

message, no change anyone else sees, no credential used, nothing the agent's

memory or workspace keeps. No built-in plugin declares it; leave it out unless

that is true of every write, and the plugin is withheld from subagents by default.

Declare sandboxForm when the service has a command-line tool. It says

what a mount of the plugin becomes inside the agent's container: the environment

variables the tool reads, the headers it sends the credential in, and the hosts

those headers may go to. The container wires in every mount that declares one

and holds a credential — found through ctx.sandboxForms(), never by a plugin's

name — so a new service's CLI works in the container with no change there. The

container only ever holds a placeholder; run9's egress proxy swaps the

credential in on requests to the declared hosts, so the agent cannot read it

there either. Two mounts that claim the same name (two GitHub accounts, one

GH_TOKEN) are both left out, and the result says why. github is the first

declarer: gh and git.

Reading a plugin's name is not always wrong; here is the test. Naming a

plugin is exactly what the deployment catalogue and a test fixture do, and they

are right to: they are saying which one to mount. It becomes a defect when the

code is picking one out of a set a third party can join. The question that

separates them: **if someone adds a second plugin that does this same thing

tomorrow, does this line pick the wrong one?**

The console's container panel filtered mounts by m.plugin === "sandbox", over

the agent's whole mount list — which anyone's plugin can enter — so the second

container-providing plugin would have silently vanished from the panel. It asks

provides now (#501). AgentRuntime.DEFAULT_MOUNTS names sandbox and is

right to, because a catalogue's whole job is to say which one. A bench harness

that mounts a plugin and then meters that same alias is naming its own fixture:

nothing can enter a set of one it built itself a few lines earlier in the same

harness (benchSweStart mounts it, benchSweStats meters it).

Note what the test is not: "is this in the kernel". cf/src/ is production

code too, and the bench line is fine there for a reason that has nothing to do

with where it lives.

**Declare reads: "parked-result" on a tool that can read a parked result

back.** A result too large for the conversation is stored and replaced with a

reference, and the runtime finds the tool that opens one by this declaration —

not by the plugin id plus a tool name. Without it, a large result is truncated

instead of parked, and nothing says why.

reads: "own-state" is the state plugin's get, and nobody else's. It

says the tool returns a value the agent itself stored, so the runtime holds

its result to the read-back line instead of parking it at 4 KiB: parked, the

agent only reads the same bytes back with one more call (limitForCall,

cf/src/runtime.ts). The runtime trusts the declaration and never looks at

what the tool returns or what it is called, so test/console-mounts.ts pins

the exact list of tools that declare it; secret_get stays parked by not

declaring it. Adding a second declarer means changing that list, with a

reason.

Show the model an image with a view field, never with its bytes. A result

object may carry one reserved top-level field, `view: { ref, mimeType, width?,

height?, bytes }: ref an artifact://` reference the calling agent can read,

mimeType one of image/png, image/jpeg, image/webp, image/gif, bytes

the stored size exactly (at most 1.5 MiB, 1,572,864, inclusive), and no other key. The harness

reads the artifact, checks its header against mimeType and its sides against

8192 pixels, and attaches it to the result for a model that can see images; a

model that cannot is told so in the result's text, and a marker that fails a

check is a sentence saying which. It is the harness's field: it is taken off

before the size decision, so it never makes a result parked, and the model

reads the rest of the result as JSON with a line about the image after it

(src/runtime/tool-view.ts). Base64 in a result is a megabyte in the

operations row and in every later request of the conversation. Only a call's

result (or a resumed question's) is read for view: a backgrounded job's result

reaches the model as a message, and a view in it is shown as plain JSON.

Work that outlives one call is backgrounded. Return

backgrounded(handle, note) from invoke, and declare `background: { poll,

cancel }`. The handle is stored as given, so it must never carry a credential.

poll runs with the same context as the call. When cancel returns, the work

must actually have stopped; if the plugin cannot confirm that, it throws.

Swallowing that failure reports a cancellation that did not happen.

A plugin whose tools come from a server lists them per mount. Declare

tools: [], mountTools(mount) and snapshotTools(ctx) (mcp.ts is the

one that does). snapshotTools asks the server and is called only when the

mount is added (/admin/mounts) and when an operator asks for a refresh

(POST /admin/mounts {tenantId, agentId, refreshTools: alias}) — never on a

call (the one turn-time exception, a snapshot under another toolsBasis, is

below). The kernel admits the list (names an agent

can address, each once; reads dropped; anything unrecognised made a write;

every tool replay: "never"; at most MAX_SNAPSHOT_TOOLS tools, each within a

description and a schema bound, all within a total — the constants and their

reasons are in src/runtime/mount-tools.ts, and a tool over a bound is skipped

with the reason, never shortened), hashes it and keeps it on the mount record

as its ToolSnapshot, replacing it only when the hash moved. A list that

cannot be fetched or cannot be stored leaves the mount as it was and is

reported to the operator who asked. mountTools reads that record and must not reach the

server. Every reader that is about one mount — the catalogue, the gateway,

tools.search/describe, the console's tool column — asks `toolsOf(plugin,

mount), never plugin.tools`, so they all see one list. Names the kernel left

out are in the snapshot's skipped, shown on the mount's console page and in

tools.mounts, and told to a model that calls one anyway. An entry is about

the one tool it names; a plugin whose list is empty for one reason that is not

about any tool (raft's mount with no credential, or one Raft refused) marks the

entry every: true (SkippedTool), and its reason then answers a call to any

of the plugin's tools the mount leaves out, its name being only a label. The version pin is still the plugin's version: a snapshot

changes what a mount offers, not which code runs it.

**Declare toolsBasis when the build decides which tools a listing can

return.** A snapshot is taken only on the occasions above, so a deploy that adds

a tool reaches no mount whose list already exists. toolsBasis is a static

string that moves whenever the set of tools this build can offer moves (raft's

is a digest of each tool's name and the capabilities it needs, so an SDK

upgrade that adds an operation or changes what one needs, a change to

EXCLUDED or a build with or without object storage changes it). The kernel

stores it on every snapshot it takes (ToolSnapshot.basis), and when a turn

starts, before the agent's tool list is built (AgentRuntime.postMessage with

prompt → retakeStaleSnapshots, cf/src/runtime.ts; never on a steer, a

follow-up, a job's delivery or a read that opens the harness, none of which

may wait on a far end; two turn starts at once share one pass; an inbound

event's turn waits too when a basis moved, since it starts in the alarm pass

after the service's request was answered), lists again any mount whose

snapshot's basis is not its plugin's — a snapshot with no basis counts as

different; a mount with no snapshot is left alone, being offered every tool

already; a switched-off plugin is skipped. The listing is bounded by

RETAKE_TIMEOUT_MS (3 s), and so is the turn's wait for the whole pass: past

it the turn runs on the list as it stands, the pass goes on in the background

(tools.retake_timeout in the log), and the next turn is built from what it

lands; a message whose harness offers tools the conversation's previous message

did not names the added ones at its head, once (src/runtime/tools-seen.ts).

When the listing fails or runs out, the old list stays —

mountTools still reads it against the running build, so a tool the build

removed is not offered — the reason goes where every failed listing's does

(snapshotError, on the mount's page), and the mount is not tried again for

that basis for RETAKE_BACKOFF_MS (10 minutes, kept in the object's memory).

A list taken under a credential is kept, and the re-take reported as failed,

when the credential did not resolve at that moment, when the far end refused it

(the listing says so with ListedTools.refused), or when the list came back

empty; an operator's refresh and a credential change still take such an answer

as it is. And a re-take writes nothing if the mount's list was replaced while it

ran (a credential attached, replaced or removed, an operator's refresh): the

newer list stands. A plugin whose list

depends only on its server (mcp.ts) declares no basis: nothing in the build

changes that list.

**Declare toolsDue when part of a list comes from a far end that moves on its

own.** toolsDue(mount, now) is synchronous and local, like mountTools: it

reads the record and whatever the plugin remembers in memory, never a server.

When it answers true for a mount that has a snapshot, the same turn-start pass

lists the mount again, with the same timeout, back-off and keep-a-credentialed-list

rules as a basis that moved (snapshotStale, cf/src/runtime.ts; issue #937),

except that an inbound event's turn does not wait for it: that re-take runs in

the background and reaches the turn after.

Raft's managed MCP is the user: its catalogue changes when a person adds,

removes or reconnects a connector in Raft, and a call can be told it is stale.

A throw is read as not due.

Read context.offered before naming one of your own tools. It is the

names of the tools this mount offers right now, as toolsOf(plugin, mount)

answers — the list the model was given and the gateway admits calls against —

filled by the gateway (#contextFor) on a call's invoke, interrupts.resume

and cancel, background.poll and cancel, receive, reportActivity and

unmount. It is absent from snapshotTools (which is deciding the list, so the one it

would be handed is the one being replaced) and from contexts built without a

call (promptContribution, Holding.*, checkCredential); read absence as

"not reported" and fall back to your static tools, never as "offers nothing".

A plugin that writes a hint, a next step or a suffix naming one of its tools

checks the name is in it: a mount's list can be narrower than tools, because

its credential lacks a capability or its snapshot predates the build that added

the tool.

Write what only the plugin sees as a trace row, through context.trace.

The gateway hands it to every call's context when the agent's store keeps a

trace, and writes the row into the same outbox as the kernel's spans

(src/trace/outbox.ts), where it is exported and read through the agent's

trace; a console line from the object is not, and can be dropped. The kind must

be one of PLUGIN_TRACE_KINDS (anything else is not written, so a plugin cannot

add a span the kernel counts); the gateway sets the time, the agent, the span

(the call's operation id, or mount:<alias> outside one) and attrs.mount. It

never throws and never fails the call. Add a kind to that list, with what it

holds, when a plugin needs one.

A plugin whose tools depend on the credential lists them per mount too.

raft is the one that does: its tools are generated from the Raft SDK's

operation manifest (RAFT_OPERATIONS), one per operation, named by the

manifest's toolName, and run through the SDK's raft.invoke. A history read

(messages_read) never counts as seen: the SDK would record the page inside

invoke, before the runtime may park it, so it runs on a client whose state is

not saved and only receive_events attests. (A held call's question attests the

messages it shows too, but only once the model has them: at once when the

question is the model's own call's result, and for one raised inside a run_js

program — which the model is not always shown — only when the model answers

it.) It attests only what the model

was shown whole: it asks Raft for at most EVENTS_LIMIT messages (sized from

PARK_BYTES and a per-message estimate), measures its result as the runtime

does (the whole result serialised, not just the text), and shows only as many

whole messages, lowest seq first, as stay under the parking line. Only those

are acknowledged and recorded as seen: Raft's since = n acknowledges every

pending row with seq ≤ n and a batch arrives in delivery order, not seq order,

so the cursor is the smallest unshown seq less one (the largest shown when all

fit), and Raft hands the rest out again first. That cursor is kept under its

own key and sent exactly as computed, since a later cut can rightly put it below

the previous one and the SDK's inbox.commit never lowers its cursor. It is

spent once sent: cleared before the pull and replaced only once the pull has

answered, before anything is recorded as seen. A response lost after Raft built

it is Raft's pending batch, so after any failure the next pull acknowledges

nothing, and the worst a failure costs is that batch handed out again. A message too long to fit alone is handed over

alone and acknowledged, so the inbox moves, but not recorded as seen: the model

got a parked preview, so a send into that conversation is still held. Every client is

made with the SDK's hints: "tool", so its hints are tool calls

(messages_read({ target: "#ops" })), named by the manifest's toolName

without the mount's alias, as every other tool reference in Raft's text is. A

hint naming a tool the mount does not offer (an EXCLUDED operation, a

generated tool missing from context.offered — one the credential does not

reach, or one added after the mount's snapshot was taken — or the SDK's

code-only raft.<operation>(…)) is put in words in one place

(offeredTerms), and so is a message line's attachment suffix when the

download is not offered (modelLine); what a person wrote (a message, a

description, a title, a preview) is passed on as written, except that the names

in a message's header line (sender, channel, thread, task assignee, attachment

filename) have line breaks and other control characters replaced with a space

(namesOnOneLine), so a name cannot start a line that reads as another message.

A generated tool's outcome reaches the model as the SDK's own state and

text, with no state rewritten: a messages_search that Raft stopped at its scan budget is

incomplete (nothing found in what it searched) or results with a page

marked incomplete, and its text says older messages were not searched and gives

the continuing messages_search({ …, cursor }) call; the tool offers cursor

as the manifest does.

attachments_download_url fetches the file Raft points at into the agent's

object storage (at most ATTACHMENT_MAX_BYTES) and returns its artifact://

reference, never the URL; it is offered when the plugin is built with object

storage (createRaftPlugin({ artifacts }), as the runtime builds it) and not

at all without, falling back to attachments.download when the Server cannot mint a URL.

attachments_view keeps an attachment the same way and, when its first bytes

are a PNG, JPEG, WebP or still GIF of at most VIEW_MAX_BYTES (1.5 MiB) and

VIEW_MAX_SIDE (2048 px a side), returns the same fields plus a view (the

reference, the type read from the bytes, width, height and size) that the gateway

can hand the model as an image; anything else, an animated GIF, WebP or PNG, or an

image over a limit is refused with what it is and the reference to the copy

kept, since nothing resizes. It is listed by a snapshot exactly where the

download is and offered only beside it.

attachments_upload goes the other way: it reads a file from the agent's own

object storage (an artifact:// reference, such as sandbox save returns;

a raw r2:// reference or one outside the caller's scope is refused before

anything is read) and uploads it to Raft for one conversation through the SDK's

typed attachments.upload, with the mount's credential. At most

UPLOAD_MAX_BYTES (25 MiB) is read; the Server's own limit and the choice

between a multipart upload and an upload session are the SDK's. It posts

nothing: it returns the attachmentId to pass to messages_send in

attachmentIds. It is offered only with object storage and to a credential

holding send, and it is declared a write, so approval policy for writes

applies and a subagent is never handed it. A tool's description and every parameter description in its

schema name operations by their tool names, never by the manifest's dotted

names, SDK field paths or CLI flags (inMountTerms). Its

snapshotTools asks Raft what the mount's credential may do

(the Agent API's credential context, GET /context, read through the SDK's

routes.agent.context → credential.capabilities) and lists only the operations whose every

capability the credential holds; mountTools offers those names, with each

tool's description and schema taken from the running build, never from the

stored copy. Because the list depends on the credential, the runtime takes it

again whenever a mount's credential is attached, replaced or removed

(AgentRuntime.attachCredential/removeCredential, for a plugin that declares

both snapshotTools and a credential), so a credential that lost a scope

stops offering the scope's tools; it declares toolsBasis, so a deploy that

changes the generated set re-lists each mount at its next turn. A listing that fails leaves the stored list

as it was when a credential was behind it; a list taken with no credential

(ToolSnapshot.withoutCredential, which a mount added before its account has)

is cleared instead. A mount with no snapshot at all — one made before the

tools were generated, one whose credential was seeded rather than attached, or

one whose first listing under a credential failed — is offered

every generated tool, and Raft refuses what its credential may not do. Which

manifest operations are not offered, and why, is one table (EXCLUDED in

src/plugins/raft.ts); test/raft-plugin.ts turns red when the manifest has

an operation that is neither generated nor excluded. Arguments of an offered

operation can be withheld the same way (WITHHELD_ARGUMENTS): left out of the

tool's schema and refused at call time before anything is sent, since nothing

else checks a call against the schema. messages_read does not offer the SDK's

unread (0.13.0), which moves the agent's own read position beside

receive_events.

Unread is not a to-do. A pull hands the inbox out oldest first, a page at a

time, so a wake told to read until hasMore is false reads every channel's

backlog to reach the one DM behind it. A pull that leaves more than it shows

therefore also carries inbox: Raft's unread listing (inbox.list, which

consumes nothing) as one line per DM, thread, or conversation that @mentions

the agent, each with the messages_read call that opens it at the read

position, and one line counting the rest as background (inboxSummary). The

wake (wakeInstruction) says to read only those and never page back. Nothing

is acknowledged that the model was not shown; a pull that leaves nothing behind

makes no listing request and reads as it did before.

Raft answers that listing with a snapshotId when the page has unread

(slock#10507), and the turn's end reports it back as turnEnded on the

activity post that carries the event ending the turn: the snapshot, how the

turn ended (completed only for a model that stopped on its own; interrupted

for an abort or an outside stop; failed for an error, a timeout or the length

limit — src/runtime/activity.ts turnEnds, beside the events and never on

them), and shownUnreadTargets, exactly the conversations whose counts the

pull's inbox lines showed (renderInboxSummary writes the lines and names the

targets in one pass; a parked pull names none). Raft settles only a completed

turn's background, never a DM, an unread @mention or an unread assigned task.

Each listing is kept under the turn it was made in (caller.turnId, the id

a turn's model calls carry as attrs.turn; pi085 only), and a turn's end

names only its own turn's listings: a turn lost without an end leaves its

listings to be dropped unreported by the next end. Every turn that saw a

snapshot reports once, however it ended; the model's own

inbox_list keeps the snapshot but names nothing, and an assistant's owner

inbox, another route, keeps none. Raft's answer is the raft.turn_ended trace

row.

A send, a reply, a task claim or a task write that Raft holds for the model's

decision comes back as a question (heldCall) whose text is the SDK's own held

text, interrupt.context, passed on unchanged: its opening "Held — …" line, any

conversation card, the conversation's window, and the messages_read call that

reads it in full, in tool form; it names the call's idempotencyKey, which is

the key the answer goes ahead under. The plugin words nothing of it, so a hold

for a reason this build does not know reaches the model in the SDK's words, and

a hold for an unseen card (interrupt.reason: "unseen_card", no new messages)

never reads as a count of messages. The answer is send/proceed or drop;

what the question showed — the messages it accounts for, and its cards — is

booked as seen in the asking context when it is the model's own call, and in

the answering context when the model answers one raised elsewhere (a run_js

program), so the answer is not held again.

Since Raft SDK 0.20.0 a task claim, status change or amend attests what the

context has seen, as a send does, and Raft may hold it for newer messages; the

SDK does not load the saved state for those, so the plugin loads it before any

call that may be held. A send the model makes itself (origin "model", never a

run_js program's) to a channel or DM right after its context read one of that

conversation's threads is stopped before sending (`interrupt.reason:

"thread_target", nothing sent, nothing to book as seen). send` posts it

top-level as written, under the same key, with the interrupt's resume.with

(targetConfirmed: true); drop sends nothing; sending to the thread instead is

a new call. The check compares reads, so each history read's target and order

are carried into the mount's saved state (never as seen).

Conversation cards (the SDK's conversationCards, 0.18.0) are the mount's

conversationCards setting, a boolean that is off unless set to true; every

client the plugin makes for that mount is then created with the option, and no

other is. It and timeoutMs are changeable, so an operator turns cards on or

off for one agent with PATCH /admin/mounts (docs/runbook.md, "Trying

conversation cards on an agent"); serverUrl is not. On, a history read starts with the channel's card (or a DM peer's)

the first time in a context, and a send into a conversation whose card the

context has not seen is held once with that card. A context is the one the

call is made in (PluginContext.caller.contextId): the SDK applies its card

rules to the frontier view invoke builds from that id, so the plugin never

sets a context on the frontier itself and two sessions never share one. A call

made in no session's turn (an approved call's replay) has no context: it is not

held for a card, and a send makes one extra history read to show the

conversation's cards after its receipt. A history read's cards are carried into

the mount's saved state, its messages are not (a read never counts as seen), and

only when the result is not parked, since a parked page's preview may not reach

them.

The plugin writes three trace rows of its own (context.trace): raft.held

for each held call (status its reason; attrs the tool, reason, target,

newMessageCount, the kinds of card it showed, contextBytes, the origin, and

for thread_target the threadTarget), and

raft.held_answer for each answer (status send, proceed, drop or

expired; ms from when the call was first held; attrs.reason the hold's

reason; attrs.outcome what came of it), both under the call's operation; and raft.state_failed when the

SDK's saved state did not load or save (the SDK's onStateError; status and

attrs.phase the phase, load or save; attrs the error and, for a save,

stateBytes, the size of the record it tried to write), which it also says on

the console. Before Raft SDK 0.19.0 this row was raft.state_save_failed. A

failed load or save never fails the call: it costs at most a batch delivered

again or one more hold. A fourth, raft.mcp_call, is one managed MCP call's end

(below). raft.turn_ended is Raft's answer to one turn's turnEnded: status

settled, skipped or unreadable (an answer that says neither, which fails

nothing), attrs.outcome how the turn ended, attrs.reason Raft's reason for a

skip, attrs.targets how many conversations were named and

attrs.snapshotMismatches listings of the turn that carried another snapshot

id; never a conversation's name.

Raft's managed MCP (src/plugins/raft-mcp.ts, SDK 0.21.1's raft.mcp) puts

the connectors the agent's Raft Server has connected (Notion and the like) on the

raft mount, as tools of their own. Raft holds the connectors' credentials and

attaches them to each call; the mount sends only its own Raft credential, to its

own origin. It is part of the raft mount rather than a mount of its own because

everything it needs is that mount's: the identity and credential, the origin, the

capability list the listing already reads, and the re-take on a credential

change.

  • Listing. When the credential holds the mcp capability, snapshotTools

also asks for the catalogue (raft.mcp.tools(), `GET

/internal/agent-api/mcp/tools`) and lists one tool per entry after Raft's own:

mcp_ and the entry's runtimeName with every character outside

[A-Za-z0-9_] made _ (_2, _3, … for a name already given), so the model

is offered <alias>__mcp_…; the entry's description and inputSchema as they

came; sideEffects: "write", idempotency: "none", whatever the remote

annotations say. The kernel's admission bounds apply, so a tool whose

description is over MAX_DESCRIPTION_BYTES is left out with the reason, not

shortened. mountTools offers the snapshot's mcp_ entries whole. What a call

needs beyond the schema (the server's id, the config and assignment versions

Raft checks) is kept in the mount's database (context store, key mcp),

written only once the kernel keeps the listing as the mount's list

(ListedTools.onKept, which the gateway runs after it writes the snapshot or

finds it unchanged, and never for a listing it sets aside), so a call goes out

with the versions of the list it was offered from. The catalogue's clock is

marked at the same moment, so a listing set aside does not put off the next.

A catalogue Raft cannot answer does not fail the listing: the tools kept from the

last one stay, and a skipped entry says why.

  • Freshness. Besides the runtime's own reasons to list, the plugin's

toolsDue makes a turn's start list again when this isolate last listed the

catalogue MCP_CATALOG_TTL_MS (5 minutes) ago or more — an isolate that has

not listed it reads the snapshot's takenAt — and at once after a call was

answered stale. A turn keeps the tools it started with; the next one is

built from the new list.

  • Reconnect. Each connector the catalogue names in reconnectRequired is one

line of the mount's prompt paragraph, in the SDK's words

(formatMcpReconnectRequired), so the agent tells the person instead of

saying it has no such connector. Beside them, each catalogue tool the

kernel's admission leaves out of the mount's list (a description over

MAX_DESCRIPTION_BYTES, a schema over MAX_SCHEMA_BYTES, a list already

full) is one line, "<server>": "<tool>" is not available here (<rule>)., by

server and remote tool name only, never the description; at most

MCP_UNAVAILABLE_LINES (20) and one line counting the rest. The listing

works them out by running the kernel's own admission (admitTools) on the

list it returns. Every connector-supplied name in these lines (a server's, a

remote tool's) is the SDK's prompt form (formatMcpPromptName, which

formatMcpReconnectRequired uses too): control and format characters made a

space, one line, cut at 80 characters, quoted as a JSON string, so a name

cannot start a line of its own in the system prompt. On pi085 a change reaches a conversation under way as a

notice (src/runtime/prompt-context.ts).

  • Calls and results. A call sends the catalogue entry and the model's

arguments once (raft.mcp.call), with a 60 s bound (MCP_CALL_TIMEOUT_MS)

rather than the mount's timeoutMs: Raft's gateway aborts the remote

server's connect and call together after about 20 s and then answers, and a

call cut short near that would read as outcome_unknown while Raft completes

or answers it. The SDK sets no timeout of its own. Nothing above the plugin

bounds the wait (the gateway sets no deadline; run_js's dynamic worker

counts CPU time). A result is the tool result (content,

and structuredContent when there is one). A tool_error is a failed call

whose message is the remote tool's text, capped by the SDK at 4,000 characters

with …[truncated]. A failure's message is the SDK's error.nextAction

exactly (mcpFailureKind): stale also marks the catalogue due;

reconnect_required and unavailable are failed calls; outcome_unknown (the

connection to Raft failed mid-call, a gateway answered 5xx with no Raft code,

or Raft's own managed_mcp_internal_error) is recorded as an operation whose outcome

is unknown.

  • No retries. The SDK makes one attempt (managedMcpCall is a single-attempt

route) and the plugin calls once. The gateway starts an operation once and

answers a second start under the same key already_attempted without reaching

the plugin, which is what a run_js program re-run after a restart meets, since

its calls are keyed ${toolCallId}:${n}. A model's own call is not re-run by

either engine after a crash (replayPolicy "never"; pi-durable unsafe):

it gets an error result instead. Tool calls do not go through a queue, so

nothing redelivers one.

  • Trace. The gateway's tool.call row, as for every call (attrs.tool

raft.mcp_…, attrs.name the name the model called it by, the mount, the

task, the call id, and for a failed call its errorCode, httpStatus and

redacted, bounded error; never arguments or a result),

and raft.mcp_call on the same span: status result, tool_error, the

failure kind or error; attrs.server the connector's server name and

attrs.tool the remote tool's name. Nothing else is recorded.

  • Subagents are not offered the tools: they are writes, and raft does not

declare actsAs: "self" (childMayCall). An agent without a raft mount, or

whose credential lacks mcp, has none.

How to check one agent: [runbook.md](runbook.md#raft-managed-mcp-on-an-agent).

Four of raft's hand-written tools are Raft Agent Login for Connected Apps

(src/plugins/raft-agent-login.ts, the steps of Raft's CLI integration list,

integration login and integration invoke): integrations_list lists the

apps installed on the agent's Raft Server with their service ids, whether the

agent has an active login and their manifest URLs (built-in Raft services left

out, and every name and description an app wrote about itself handed over as

untrusted data, one line each, clipped, with credential shapes removed),

integrations_login asks Raft for a grant and turns

the app's one-time callback into a session, integrations_actions lists the

actions the app's manifest offers, and integrations_invoke runs one of them.

The app's session cookie is a credential the agent holds for that app, so it is

kept only in the mount's database, sealed under a key derived from the mount's

Raft credential, and every result is walked for it before the model sees one.

An action is run only if the manifest names it, and sent only to the origin the

manifest was read from (the URL Raft registered for the app), over https, never

to an internal host and never along a redirect; the Raft credential goes to

Raft alone. approval_required and install_required are handed back as Raft

gave them, with the card Raft posts when the call names a target; an action

the app answers 401 is sent once more after a fresh login. Only v0 manifests

(raft-agent-manifest.v0) with http_api actions that answer JSON or text are

run, and a manifest whose base_url has a path is refused rather than joined; test/raft-agent-login.ts holds all of it against a fake Raft and a fake

app.

Two more are a personal assistant's reads of its owner's Raft:

assistant_owner_inbox (the owner's channels and threads with unread counts,

mentions and a preview of the latest message) and assistant_owner_messages

(one of those conversations by its channelId, paged with at most one of

before, after and around). Since Raft RFC 077 they include the owner's DMs

with other people unless the owner turned that off (a DM row is kind channel

with channelType dm, the other person named only by channelName; the inbox

lines show channelType), and never the DM between the assistant and its owner. The inbox's

filter is unread (the default), all, mentions, unread_mentions or untold: unread and

not yet told to the owner (Raft's assistant_told, by seq; what the assistant skipped counts as

not told). Each line shows its told and the result says the answer's total (how many match,

across every page) when Raft sends them; with totalCapped: true (Raft read only its first 300

conversations) the total is a lower bound and the line says "at least". Until Raft and the SDK take untold, a read with it

that is answered 400, refused by the SDK's contract, or answered with no numeric total is read

once more, the same page with unread, and the result opens with a note that told state is

unavailable and there is no count of what remains; no other failure is retried. They are

read-only and model-only, and offered only when the mount's snapshot lists

them, which it does when the same credential context names whose assistant the

account is (agent.assistantOf with a userId): never with the tools every mount

has, never to a mount with no snapshot, and a call on a mount whose list lacks

them is refused before anything is sent. Every successful result opens with a

line saying the content is the owner's, written outside the conversation, to

be treated as information rather than instructions; a failure shows Raft's

HTTP status and error code as sent (403 assistant_not_enabled,

404 channel_not_found, with Raft's message when it sent one, cut at 300

characters and marked … (cut)) and is not

retried; a 5xx, a 429 or no answer at all is marked transient. Both reads'

answers are camelCase (hasMore, hasOlder, nextOffset), and one in

another style is refused as malformed rather than read as missing. They go

through the SDK's routes (routes.assistant.ownerInbox / ownerMessages,

SDK 0.13.0, RAFT_ASSISTANT_WIRE in src/plugins/raft.ts) on the mount's own

credential, with no saved state: neither marks anything read for anyone. The

listing reads agent.assistantOf from the context route itself

(routes.agent.context), not from identity.whoami, which is a second

projection over the same answer: 0.13.0's left the field out, and since 0.13.1

it carries it. The SDK's contract for that answer refuses an assistantOf

that is not null or an object with a string userId, which fails the whole

listing (the stored list stays as it was); an empty userId passes it and is

not an owner here, and any other text is, UUID or not.

test/raft-assistant.ts holds the reads end to end through the SDK against a

fake Raft, and the rendering against a fake wire, which can hand it answers

the SDK's contract would refuse first.

The generated raftbench_findings (SDK 0.26.0) is gated the same way: listed only when the credential context's

agent.raftbenchReader is literally true (slock #10609; absent or any other value is not a reader), never offered

to a mount with no snapshot, refused as an unknown tool where the mount's list lacks it, and a 403 from Raft has the

mount listed again at the next turn so a revoked grant drops it (isRaftbenchReader, RAFTBENCH_READER_BASIS).

Since SDK 0.28 a page with a nextCursor ends with its next page as a call (the SDK's next: the same filters

and limit with the cursor, findingsText). A failure's next action is the Server's suggestedNextAction as it came

(slock #10733: read the first page again without a cursor, or tell the person who asked); a failure the SDK's

raftbenchFailureKind calls stop (raftbench_findings_unavailable, a reader no longer allowed) is marked as one

that will not clear, even on a 503 (findingsFailure). Its channelId names channels_info, which a reader's read

credential may lack, only "if that tool is available" (SDK 0.28.1), the one way a description may name such a tool.

Such an agent is also proactive (Raft RFC 075 §4.2, src/plugins/raft-assistant.ts),

and code decides before any model is asked. A push notice that names RFC 074's

doorbell (a system.assistant event) is answered { deliver: false, wake: true }

(InboundResult), so the runtime asks the mount's wake and the model is given

nothing; a notice that also carries the owner's DM is delivered to the chat as

well. The wake reads the owner's inbox itself. Raft serves that inbox from a

view that lags the facts the doorbell rings from, so when the doorbell's event

says when its change happened (first_change_at, read by the notice's

agent-event: address with consume: false) and no row is that recent (2 s of

slack) in the unread read nor in a read of every conversation (which shows a

change the owner already read; asked only then), the read decides nothing: no model call, nothing recorded as looked at,

and the inbox is read again after 5, 20 and 60 s (INBOX_LAG_RETRY_MS), then 2, 5

and 15 minutes, after which the wake gives up on the change

(inbox_lagging_gave_up) and decides on what it read without recording it as

looked at. Otherwise it drops what a check already

looked at (per conversation: one no check has looked at is new however old its

activity), what the owner was already told and the DM between the assistant and

its owner, then applies the owner's contact rules (below) and the defaults; with

nothing left it ends there. It then reads each conversation left,

once more on a failure; one that still cannot be read is not handed to the

model, is not recorded as looked at, is named in the DM's last line ("I

couldn't read …; I'll look again shortly") once, until its read succeeds or it

has new activity, and brings wakes back after 2, 5 and 15 minutes, then waits

for the next doorbell. With nothing readable there is no model call and that

line is all the owner gets. With something left it posts one turn

with those conversations as input in a short session of its own

(PluginNext.isolated, src/runtime/isolated-session.ts): a new session that

sees nothing of the owner's chat, is offered only the reads and

assistant_notify_owner, and is erased once its run ends. The same wake is the

morning brief at 08:00 owner-local (Raft's assistantOf.timezone, else the zone

the owner gave, else UTC), re-armed every time it runs, and skipped without a

model call on a morning with nothing unread, nothing active and nothing held.

Every wake writes a raft.assistant_wake trace row and a per-day count, its

status one of skipped_empty, skipped_filtered, invoked, unreadable,

inbox_lagging, paused, night_deferred or failed, with an unreadable

count, and, on a doorbell that said when its change happened, lagRetry (the

reads before this one that did not show it), caught_up, and caught_up_via

(unread or all); a failed (or paused) row's error is a code (inbox_refused with the read's HTTP

status, inbox_shape, not_enabled, unexpected), never an error's text, which can name a conversation.

Each assistant_notify_owner call that returns writes a

raft.assistant_notify row on its call's span (beside its tool.call row): its

state (sent, nothing_to_send, kept_for_brief, not_sent), the items given,

sent and held back, and the items dropped by category, never an item or a reference. assistant_notify_owner composes and sends one

DM to the owner, one plain line per item (summaries cut at 120 characters), at most three items with an

@mention or a DM first, then N more — reply "more" to see them.; what it holds back is not recorded as

told and its conversations are not counted as looked at, so the next wake considers them again. It sends

to assistantOf.dmTarget when Raft gives it, else to the DM learnt from the owner writing (it takes no

destination from the model, and refuses while neither is known), and keeps its rules in code: every reference is a message's

#channel:msgid (a bare task #N is refused, since in the DM it is the DM's own

task, and Raft has no channel-qualified task reference); one the check's reads

cannot confirm is replaced by the conversation's first unread message; an item already told is

dropped, as is one a rule mutes; one a digest rule covers, and everything in the owner's quiet hours but

what an anytime rule covers, is kept for the brief. A #channel:msgid the assistant sends its owner in

their chat (the answer to "more") counts as told too, so the next wake does not bring it back

(noteToldInChat, a raft.assistant_chat_told row with the count; while Raft runs the check, Raft's

Server records these itself and the plugin records nothing). The chat prompt answers "more"

and "what's new" from an inbox read with untold from the start (no offset: what was already told drops out of it), at most three lines, and says how many are left

only from the read's total minus the lines of that reply ("at least" that many when the read says so), never from its own count or memory, and with no number when the read

says told state is unavailable. Each chat turn's input says which language to write in: to the owner, Raft's

assistantOf.language as last read (a tag such as zh-CN, used only when it reads as one; null, or absent on an older Server, leaves

none), else the language of the owner's latest message; whatever is sent as the owner in another conversation, the

language of that conversation's recent messages. The assistant_send_as_owner summary has the assistant read them first

with assistant_owner_messages, by the channelId of the conversation's assistant_owner_inbox line (filter all:

a quiet conversation is not among the unread), and ask the owner which language when it cannot read them or they show no language (an empty conversation shows none), rather than

write in theirs. Both defaults give way to the owner: a language they ask for, and words they gave to send in their chat, which

go out as they are. A check's and the brief's summaries are asked for in Raft's language when it gives one.

When Raft runs the check (RFC 077 step 2), it says so in the credential context, assistantOf.raftCheck: true, and

that flag is the only switch: read at every listing, when the brief's timer fires and when a check arrives on a stale

flag (src/plugins/raft-assistant.ts). While it is true, the plugin's own brief, prefilter, inbox-lag reads,

composition and rules store are off. Raft's doorbell (owner_inbox_updated) and its brief (owner_brief) carry a

check (payload.check, src/plugins/raft-assistant-check.ts), and a wake with one posts check.instruction as the

short session's input, as written but for {{tool:notify}}, {{tool:owner_inbox}} and {{tool:owner_messages}},

which become the mount's model tool names (removed when the session lacks the tool; an unknown placeholder is left and

counted). An instruction over 16 KB of UTF-8 as posted (names put in), another version or a check with no id or

trigger is refused, never cut, and a malformed one is never kept in place of a valid pending one. allowed_tools

can only narrow the check's tools: it is intersected in code with the reads and the notify tool, the runtime offers

the session that list alone (PluginNext.tools) and refuses anything else of the mount at the host too. The notify

tool then forwards the items ({ channelId, summary, kind? } each) to POST /assistant/notify with the check's id,

from the session's key, and an idempotency key made from the check and the items; the model gives neither. A wake with

a check clears a stale pause. The chat's references are recorded as told by Raft's Server itself (the plugin sends

nothing and keeps nothing for them), the preferences tool

reads and writes Raft's contact-rules and contact-settings, and the first time the flag reads true the plugin pushes

{ briefSent } once to POST /assistant/check-state/import (from the listing that sees it, or the wake that reads it),

retried with backoff and given up after six tries (import_gave_up). Rollback: a check is posted only while the

flag reads true (a check on a flag kept false makes the wake read Raft's first); checks queued before the flag went

off are dropped (check_refused, raft_check_off), a doorbell with no check reads the flag again before it is

skipped, and a flag turned off after 08:00 owner-local counts that day as briefed. With the flag false or absent,

everything is as above.

The owner's contact rules (src/plugins/raft-assistant-rules.ts) are set through

assistant_preferences, offered in the chat: add records one rule, an effect (mute, always,

digest) about exactly one subject (a channel or a thread, kept by Raft's channelId; a person, kept by

their Raft id, which Raft's GET /assistant/owner-resolve?name= read gives at once, a 404 for the name

refusing the rule, while a Raft without that route has the inbox rows looked in instead; a kind of item;

or the owner's DMs), with an optional until (at most 90 days) and, for

always only, anytime (also in quiet hours); list gives each rule as one plain line with the

defaults and the quiet hours; remove deletes one by id or all; quiet sets the quiet hours (HH:MM

owner-local, or off; 22:00-08:00 by default); timezone keeps the zone the owner gave; mute and

unmute are the older names of add with mute and of remove. Code validates each (a channel or a

thread must be one of the owner's conversations; a name two people share is refused) and answers with a

confirm line written from the stored rule. The most specific matching rule wins (thread, then person,

then channel or DMs, then kind), and at equal specificity the newer one; what was already told or

looked at is dropped before any rule is asked. A channel's or a thread's mute or digest covers its

general chat only: an @mention of the owner there still comes, and a DM is never in a channel; muting

the person silences their @mentions and DMs. The defaults send a DM, an @mention and a followed thread

to the model; a channel's general chat is dropped in code (default_chatter) only once Raft gives the

owner's name (assistantOf.name or handle, read when present), so a message naming them without an @

is told apart; until then it goes to the model (chatter_unfiltered). A person rule is applied in code

only when inbox rows carry sender ids (latestSenderId with latestSenderType, user read as a human and a

system sender as no person, and peer: { id, type } on a DM row or a DM's thread row, read when present);

until then the check's model applies it from a line in its input, and each wake counts

person_rule_unenforced. Rules kept from before are read as this version's with their ids: a channel's

is bound to its id at the next wake that shows it, and one that named several things at once is kept as

it was and still applied in notify. In quiet hours a doorbell reads nothing unless an always rule is

marked anytime; then only what such a rule covers is handed on. Each preferences call writes a

raft.assistant_rules row (action, effect, subject kind, result such as added or

refused:channel_unknown, and the rules' counts per effect and subject kind); the wake row adds counts

such as muted_channel, muted_person, digest, always, anytime_night, night_held, dm,

own_dm, default_chatter and rules_total. No trace row or log line carries a channel's or a

person's name or id.

When the owner explicitly asks the assistant to send, reply or post something for them, it does so

with assistant_send_as_owner, offered in the chat only: Raft's POST /assistant/send-as-owner

(RFC 077; the mount's credential and its send capability, no SDK route yet) posts it under the owner's

name, labelled as sent by the assistant on their behalf, and sends the owner a receipt with a delete

button, so the assistant sends no confirmation of its own. The prompt has it send at once, with no draft

to approve, ask one short question only when what to send or to whom is unclear, and never send as the

owner unasked. The tool takes a target (#channel:msgid, which replies in that message's thread, or

with inThread: false posts in its channel; #channel; @name for the owner's DM with that person; or

a channelId from the owner inbox, a thread's own posting in that thread) and the content. A

channelId is taken as it is, with no read. A #channel (alone or in #channel:msgid) is asked of

Raft's GET /assistant/owner-resolve?name=%23<name> first, which answers a channel the owner can see in

the assistant's server, public, private or joint, however many conversations they have: a 200 whose

type is channel with a UUID id is sent to; any other 200 (a person, a body with no such id) and the

404 are a miss, never a place to send. A Raft from before channels were read by name answers #name as a

person's 404, so on a miss the channel is looked for in the owner's inbox, every page, bounded at 50

pages (2,500 rows); stopped at that bound with more left, the answer is channel_beyond_read, not

channel_unknown. Any other failure of the read (a 5xx, no answer) is resolve_failed, with no inbox

read and nothing sent. An @name is the owner's DM row with that person in the first four pages of the

inbox. A message's full id comes from a thread row whose root it is, when the inbox was read, else a read

around its short id. Code keeps, whatever the model does: a call from a short session (a check or the

brief) or from a program is refused before anything is read, and the tool is not offered there either;

@everyone and @here are refused before anything is read; one request per call, never retried, and

after a rate_limited the same message to the same place is not sent again for ten minutes; within one

turn (caller.turnId) the same message to the same place is posted once (an uncertain send counts,

since it may have landed), a second call answered

not_sent / duplicate_in_turn without a request (traced refused:duplicate_in_turn). A refusal

(assistant_send_as_owner_off, channel_not_found, message_not_found, owner_cannot_post,

mass_mention_not_allowed, invalid_content, rate_limited, channel_archived, a missing route, a

target that cannot be resolved, channel_unknown being "there's no channel by that name that you can see

in this server", or resolve_failed) is answered not_sent with the code and a tell line for the owner,

followed by the message where they could send it themselves; no answer, or a 5xx, is uncertain,

without the text, since it may have landed. Each call writes a raft.assistant_send_as_owner row:

result (sent, uncertain, failed, resolve_failed or refused:<code>, refused:proactive among

them), target_kind (message, channel, dm, channel_id), in_thread, chars (the message's

length), Raft's HTTP status as http_status, and how the target was resolved: resolved_by (id,

resolve, inbox, or none when it was not), with rows_read, pages_read and bound_hit wherever

the inbox was read, and for a #channel target or a channelId resolve_status: the HTTP status Raft

answered its read by name with (200 for any answer), null when Raft was not asked or gave no status; never

the message, a name or an id. The trace export forwards only the fields the receiver keeps, which do not

include these five yet.

An assistant's one Raft app is the Reminder app (Raft offers an assistant no other, slock#10764), used

through assistant_reminder, offered in the chat only (src/plugins/raft-assistant-reminder.ts) and

built on the Raft SDK's raft.integrations (SDK 0.29). It takes an action and its params, nothing

else: called with no action it returns the app's actions and their parameters from its manifest

(integrations.actions, no login); with one it calls it (integrations.call, which logs in through

Raft, opens the app's callback for its session cookie, and calls the action on the app's registered

origin, never following a redirect). Code keeps, whatever the model does: the app is never the model's

to name. A service (or any other argument) is refused, and the Reminder app is found in Raft's list

(integrations.list), picked by Raft's official mark with a purpose about reminders, else by its name,

and called by the service id Raft gave; no Reminder app listed (a deployment with none configured)

answers unavailable once, with nothing logged in or called. Only an action the manifest names is

called, and its params only as the manifest declares them (no parameter it does not name, every one it

requires, each of its type); anything else is refused before anything reaches the app. A call from a

short session (a check or the brief) or a program is refused in code, and the tool is not offered there

either. What the model reads is the SDK's: the app's answer, marked as the app's data (result), the

SDK's sentence for approval_required or install_required (a person has to act; nothing ran), or a

failure's text and next action (Raft's refusal such as assistant_integration_not_allowed with Raft's

next action, an app's refusal as APP_ERROR with the app's words quoted), with the SDK's mentions of

its own integrations.* methods said as this tool; nothing is retried. The app's session cookies are

the agent's credential at that app: they are kept (the SDK's integrationSessions) in the mount's own

database in the agent's Durable Object, store integrationSessions, one row per app client id holding

the Raft agent id it belongs to, sealed (AES-GCM) under a key derived from the mount's Raft credential

and bound to both ids, listed nowhere; a row for another agent, or one that does not open, reads as

none and the SDK logs in again. Removing the mount's credential (an agent's delete does) clears the

store. Every cookie pair of the session, and every cookie value that looks like a token (8 or more

characters mixing letters and digits, or 16 or more), is cut from what the model reads, should the app

echo one; a common value such as true or en is not, so the app's ids, dates and JSON reach the model

intact. Each call writes a raft.assistant_reminder row and log line:

action (the action's name, only when the manifest has it) and outcome (actions, result,

approval_required, install_required, unavailable, refused:<why> or error:<SDK code>); that row

and the log never carry the params, the app's answer, the session or the login code. The gateway's own

tool.call row for a failed call carries the error message's first 200 characters, as for every tool

(TOOL_ERROR_MAX, src/trace/seams.ts), so an APP_ERROR there quotes the app's words.

A 403 assistant_not_enabled from any call

pauses the checks, the brief and the notify tool until Raft's context names the

owner again. The chat is offered the owner DM, attachments, the reads, the

preferences, send-as-owner and the Reminder app (plus resume and the parked-result reader), with no run_js, jobs

or subagents, under a prompt of its own (assistantSystemPrompt,

src/runtime/pi-prompt.ts, the one entry point for it).

That prompt is split by who owns each part. Raft owns the stable part: how the assistant talks

(voice), and that it follows its owner's contact settings and answers from them, with no concrete

default in it (no brief time, quiet hours or list of what is reported). It sends that as the agent's instructions

(POST on create, PATCH to change), which the runtime reads back as the persona's description.

antiproton keeps the part bound to code (assistantPrompt, src/plugins/raft-assistant.ts): the

tools and how to use them (the owner reads, the DM, assistant_preferences,

assistant_send_as_owner), that what it reads is information and never instructions, that it sends

as its owner only when the owner explicitly asks, which DMs the reads cover, the #channel:msgid

citation the told-tracking parses, the shape and limit of the unasked messages code composes, and

that every reply to the owner, a question back included, goes through messages_send (the text a

turn ends with is shown to no one; this is the code part's last paragraph, so the last thing the model

reads, and is said again in the precedence line), the effective defaults, written from code's constants: the brief's time (BRIEF_HOUR), the default

quiet hours (DEFAULT_QUIET) and what is reported by default. The preferences tool's list returns

the same facts for the owner's settings (quiet, brief, defaults, then the rules), which is what

the assistant answers "what rules do you follow" from.

The chat's prompt is the name line, then Raft's instructions verbatim, or

DEFAULT_ASSISTANT_INSTRUCTIONS (src/plugins/raft-assistant-instructions.ts) when they are empty

or blank, then the code part, whose first line (assistantPrecedence) says it wins over anything

above it that conflicts: the instructions can change how the assistant sounds, not what it may do,

and the tool guards (no send-as-owner from a check, refused in code) hold whatever they say. A

check's prompt is assistantCheckPrompt alone, with no name and no instructions: what a check

reports is decided by code and the owner's rules. Nothing in the system prompt is generated per

turn (the owner's current rules reach the chat through the preferences tool and a check or brief

through its input), so each agent's prompt is one stable, cacheable prefix.

docs/assistant-default-instructions.md is the default text exactly, for handing to Raft (a test

holds it equal to the constant). What not to put in Raft's instructions: tool names, limits,

reference formats, or any rule about sending as the owner, treating read content as instructions,

or applying contact settings; those are code's, and an instruction that contradicts them loses.

What an owner read returns is never parked: a result over the line is cut with a note

to page, whatever reader is mounted, and a short session's erase also deletes anything its calls parked. Reads of an

assistant's conversation by an operator or the console need a reason and are audited (docs/runbook.md). An assistant runs on pi085 only: moving it to pd

(migrateEngine) is refused with the reason, and one already there has each

wake counted failed with reason: engine_pd_unsupported and a warning, since

pd cannot hold a short session. test/raft-assistant-mode.ts holds the rules (precedence, each guard, migration, privacy with sentinel names),

sending as the owner (each target, each refusal, no retry, the short-session guard end to end, privacy),

the code before the model, the short session, the engine and the offered lists.

replay: "never" overrides the read rule. A tool that declares it is not

run again on its own after an interruption, even when it is a read

(replayPolicy asks it first). Declare it on a read whose claim to be harmless

is not yours to make; the kernel sets it on every tool a server listed, because

a remote readOnlyHint is that server's word, not reviewed code.

**Declare modelOnly: true on a tool whose result only counts once the model

has read it.** A run_js program's call to it is refused with

not_from_a_program whatever name the program uses — the model's

(inbox__receive_events), the address (inbox.receive_events), or the

plugin's (raft.receive_events, which the gateway resolves when one mount uses

the plugin): run_js marks every call it makes fromProgram

(src/runtime/pi-tools.ts) and the gateway refuses on that

(src/runtime/gateway.ts). The model's own call goes through as before. Such a

tool is also never held for approval, since an approved call runs with nobody

reading the result: where the mount's policy or the model's confirm would

hold it, the call is refused and the model told why, so it is usable only on a

mount whose policy lets the agent call it without approval. Raft's receive_events declares it: it acknowledges the

previous batch and records what it hands over as seen, so from a program the

model would have acknowledged, and attested to having read, messages it never

saw. (messages_read does not: Raft marks a history read as read, so every

call but the model's own in its turn — no caller.fromProgram, a

caller.contextId, both below — runs as code, which the SDK reads with

consume: false and books as seen nowhere. That covers a program's call and

an approved call's replay. A Raft tool generated from an operation the

manifest marks model-only carries the flag, so the gateway refuses it before

the SDK's own MODEL_ONLY refusal is reached.) A tool a server lists never

carries it (the kernel admits only the fields it knows).

Read context.caller for where a call came from. Besides tenantId,

agentId and taskId it carries two read-only fields, filled by the gateway

on both engines and present only when they say something:

  • fromProgram: true — the call came from a run_js program, not from the

model's own tool call. It is InvokeOpts.fromProgram, which run_js sets

after the program's options, so a program can neither clear it nor set it on

someone else's call. Absent for the model's calls, an approved call's replay,

provisioning and bench shells. Use it for a tool a program may call but whose

result the model has not necessarily seen; a tool a program must never reach

declares modelOnly instead.

  • contextId — an opaque id for the model's current context window in this

agent's session. It is the same on every call of one context and never per

turn, and a restarted object computes the same value, because it is derived

from durable state (src/runtime/context-id.ts). It changes on exactly

these: a new session, a compaction, a reset, a move between engines, and a

transcript that was remade — and, on pi085, a branch summary, which nothing

writes today. Two ways a read can leave the context do not

change it, and neither drops a read today: pi085 moving the conversation's

tip back (navigateTree, used only to resume a caller's paused functions,

which carries every result along) and pi-durable's context edits (omit /

replace, which nothing writes; a test fails if something starts to). Scope

"what the model has seen" by it and compare it for equality only. Absent

when the call is not made in a session's turn (an approved call's replay,

provisioning, a bench shell, a background job's poll): treat that as

"nothing is known to be seen".

taskId is the conversation the call was made in: main for the agent's

own, an Agents API session's id, or sub_… for a subagent's

(docs/agent-surface.md, "Subagents"). A subagent shares the agent's mounts, so

its calls reach a plugin as the agent's do, with its own contextId: a plugin

that keys what the model has seen by conversation keeps a subagent's apart from

its parent's.

Use context.operationId as the idempotency key you hand a service. It is

the gateway's id for the operation the context serves, set by the gateway

alone on both engines (src/runtime/gateway.ts, #contextFor); compare it

for equality only. What stays the same and what does not:

PathoperationId the plugin sees
The model's own tool call (invoke)a new id per call; the model calling again is a new id
The call held for approval, run after a person approves it (applyApproval)the id the call was held under, which the model was told
interrupts.resume of a question the call asked, and of each question after itthe asking call's id (the resume is recorded as a step of its own, under its own row)
interrupts.cancel of that question (dropped, expired, asked where nobody can answer, or in a shape no answer can meet)the asking call's id
background.poll and background.cancel of work the call started (the alarm, the jobs tool, a session cancel, the time ceiling, the cap)the call's id (the runtime keeps a job under it)
A run_js program's callderived from the run_js call's id and the call's position in the program (${toolCallId}:${n}): different per call, and the same if the program is run again under the same run_js call, where the gateway finds the operation already begun and answers already_attempted without reaching you again
promptContribution, Holding.*, receive, reportActivity, snapshotTools, unmount, a bench runner's poll of a job it holds only the handle ofabsent: no operation

Nothing a model or a program writes reaches it: run_js builds each call's

options field by field and the production host forwards no operationId

(hostCallOpts, cf/src/runtime.ts), and the arguments are the tool's own.

Two things it does not do. One operation can make more than one write — a

call and then its resume, say — so if more than one of them must land, tell

them apart yourself (suffix the id). And it does not survive a recovery: after

a crash both engines re-run only a replay-safe call (a read, or a write

declaring idempotency: "native"), and the re-run is a new operation with a

new id, so a write that relies on this id for its deduplication must not

declare itself native on that ground.

Bound a number, shape a header list. A number setting can carry min and

max; a value outside them is refused when the mount is written, as everything

in validateMount is — refused, not clamped, so the person writing it learns

the limit. A string[] of HTTP headers takes format: "header-lines"

(headerLines in src/plugins/types.ts): "Name: value" lines, no header the

client writes itself, and no credential written out. A setting is public, so a

value with no {{name}} slot is refused under a header that carries a

credential by convention (Authorization, Cookie, X-Api-Key, …) or when it

reads as Bearer …/Basic …/Token … under any name; keep the value as a

secret and write Bearer {{name}}. Read the value through the same function at

call time, so a value stored before a rule existed is refused there too.

A rule config cannot say goes in configProblem(config). Return the

reason as a string, or undefined to accept. validateMount asks it once every

setting is declared, typed and present, so it runs wherever settings are judged:

a mount added from the console or /admin/mounts, a seed in provisioning, a

reconcile, a credential being attached, and the console's problems column. It is

synchronous and must not reach a server.

consoleMount: true lets an agent's owner add a mount from the console.

Opt-in (mcp is the one that does): the console builds a form from config,

one field per setting, and POST /ui/mount/add coerces each (configFromForm

in src/runtime/mount-config.ts) before the usual checks. Declare it only when

every setting is one a stranger may type, and a mount is safe to delete: the

console can also refresh and remove the mounts it added (at most eight per

agent), refusing a removal while the mount holds a credential, a held call or

running work (the last two only for a plugin that holds or backgrounds

something; mcp does neither, so a call already in flight finishes and the

next one is refused with not_mounted). A live inbound hook does not refuse

it: the runtime revokes the mount's hooks itself, after unmount (below).

unavailable() says the deployment cannot run the plugin at all. It

takes no context, because the answer is the same for every agent: return a

reason when configuration the plugin was built with, and no mount can supply,

is missing (a service's origin, the deployment's client credential), and

null otherwise. Provisioning does not seed a catalogue row whose plugin is

unavailable, so a new agent is not handed tools whose every call would

refuse; a mount that already exists stays, and its calls should refuse with

the same reason. Absent means always available. reminder is the first

declarer: it is unavailable without REMINDER_APP_ORIGIN and

REMINDER_APP_CREDENTIAL.

**unmount(ctx) is a plugin's one chance to clean up when its mount is

removed.** Removing a mount deletes what is filed under its alias here — its

database, its tool list, its hooks — and nothing the plugin registered

elsewhere: a webhook created through the service's API, a subscription. Only

the plugin knows those, so the runtime (removeMount in cf/src/runtime.ts)

calls unmount with the mount's normal context (db, and inbound for a

plugin that receives) and the plugin cancels them there, reading the ids it

kept in ctx.db. ctx.credential is always null: a removal is refused while

an account is attached, so it has been detached by the time unmount runs. A

plugin that must authenticate to deregister uses ctx.ownerSecret or a

credential of the deployment's (detaching an account could one day call

unmount too). What the runtime promises:

  • it is called once, and only once the removal is going to happen: a

removal refused for any of the reasons above never calls it;

  • it is bounded by a timeout (UNMOUNT_TIMEOUT_MS, ten seconds), because

a person is waiting on the console;

  • a throw or a timeout does not block the removal. The mount is removed and

the reason is shown to the person who removed it (above the panel, and as

unmountError in /ui/mount/remove's JSON), since whatever it could not

cancel is now theirs to cancel by hand. A call past the deadline is not

stopped, but its context is closed: from then on every ctx.db,

ctx.inbound and other context call throws, so a late write cannot land

under the removed alias, nor in a new mount that has taken it since;

  • nothing else reaches the mount meanwhile: once the removal is decided,

tool calls on it are refused and pushed events are ignored without waking the

agent, and unmount runs under the mount's lock, after any exclusive call

already running;

  • then every hook of the mount still live is revoked — the index row first,

so the URL answers 404 at once, then its secret — so a hook the plugin forgot,

or could not reach because it failed, does not stay a public address for an

alias a later mount may take. If that revoke fails, the removal is refused

with the reason and can be tried again; unmount will have run, so make it

safe to run a second time over what it already cancelled.

It runs even when the plugin is switched off or the agent is pinned to another

version: switching off stops tools and deliveries, not the cleanup owed for what

the plugin registered while it was on. That is safe because unmount is

cleanup only — cancel registrations, subscriptions, reminders — and never

wakes the agent, posts a message or calls a model. That is a rule the plugin

keeps, not a limit the runtime enforces: the context has nothing that wakes the

agent, but sibling hands over another mount's credential and the network is

open, so a plugin could still post somewhere.

Revoking through ctx.inbound inside unmount is allowed and changes nothing

about the order; telling the service to stop sending is the part only the

plugin can do. test/plugin-unmount.ts holds these rules.

Two kinds of kept secret, and who reads which. The agent keeps its own with

state.secret_put under kept:; a plugin reaches them by name through

ctx.agentSecret, and the model can read them back (secret_get), list,

overwrite and delete them. The owner keeps theirs from the console

(POST /ui/secret) under owner:; a plugin reaches them through

ctx.ownerSecret, which the gateway hands only to a plugin that declares

readsOwnerSecrets: true, and nothing the model can call reads, lists, overwrites or

deletes them — the console shows only their names and times, and no route

returns a value. Use ownerSecret only for a value sent where the mount's own

settings say, never where a tool call's arguments say, or the agent could send

the owner's credential anywhere. mcp fills a {{name}} header from the

owner's secret of that name first, then the agent's, so the agent cannot shadow

the owner's. What a server sends back can still carry one: mcp masks every value

it filled in, verbatim, in results, errors and the tool list it stores, and a

server that echoes one encoded is not caught; a number in the tool list is read

as the text it prints as, so a digits-only secret is masked there too. The rule

is held by test/mcp-plugin.ts ("a plugin is handed ownerSecret only when it

declares readsOwnerSecrets": the gateway withholds it), and

test/console-mounts.ts keeps mcp.ts the only plugin file that names
