2 s per failed attempt before retrying, so a batch with N failed attempts waits up to N × 2 s), tool.refused (a host call

refused before an operation existed, by the gateway, run_js or the executor: status the refusal's code, attrs tool

(left out unless shaped like a tool name and carrying nothing shaped like a credential), code, by, from (model or

program), callId, task; never its arguments; per run_js run at most 16 rows from run_js and the executor, plus the

gateway's, which the run's 64-call host budget bounds: 80 in all),

mount.settings (an operator's in-place change of a mount's settings, [docs/plugins.md](plugins.md)), subagent.turn

(one turn of a [subagent](#subagents-send_to_agent) ending: spanId its session, parentId the tool call that sent the

turn, status done, failed, cancelled, stopped, report_failed, report_dropped or wake_failed (a background

completion that could not be posted, so no turn started), attrs.parentSession and, on an end, reportChars, the report

body's length; a cancelled end's attrs.cause is ceiling or aborted, a kill switch's stopped end's is switched_off), subagent.start (a child's turn beginning: spanId its session,

parentId the parent's send_to_agent call, status spawn, follow_up or wake), github.refresh (one outcome of renewing an expiring GitHub token, [docs/runbook.md](runbook.md#a-github-connection-that-needs-reconnecting): status the outcome, attrs scope (connector or mount), connectorId, mount, why, attempt, stored and reasonCode, a code from a fixed set; never a token or GitHub's own words), and the rows a plugin writes of its own, raft.held, raft.held_answer, raft.state_failed (written as raft.state_save_failed before Raft SDK 0.19.0) and raft.mcp_call (one [managed MCP](#raft-managed-mcp) call's end: status result, tool_error or the failure kind, attrs.server and attrs.tool; never arguments or a result), and raft.turn_ended (Raft's answer to a turn's turnEnded report: status settled, skipped or unreadable, attrs.outcome, attrs.reason, attrs.targets a count; never a conversation's name)

([docs/plugins.md](plugins.md)); src/trace/outbox.ts) — with from <= at < to, in seq order.

  • from, to — ISO 8601 or milliseconds since the epoch; the window is at most 24 hours. One alone

puts the other 24 hours away; neither is the 24 hours up to now. to before or at from, a wider

window, and a window reaching past ±8.64e15 ms (the last time a date holds) are 422, before the

agent is asked.

  • limit — rows per page, 1 to 1000, default 200. cursor — a seq: only rows after it, from the

previous page's nextCursor. A page may hold fewer rows than limit, none even, and still have a

nextCursor: one request reads at most 5,000 rows from the agent and 20 exported batches, and says

where it stopped. Only a null nextCursor means nothing is left.

Source and lag. A row is written to the agent's own outbox (trace_outbox) in the step that ends its

span, and the agent's next alarm pass exports it in batches of up to 500 to object storage

(trace/<tenant>/<agent>/<fromSeq>-<toSeq>.ndjson, cf/src/trace-r2.ts), then forgets it. The route

reads the outbox first and the exported batches second, so a row is found whether or not it was

exported, and one exported between the two reads is found in its batch: no lag past the span's end.

What is never there: a span still open (a model call awaiting its answer, a tool still running), and

anything the trace does not record (src/trace/outbox.ts names the documented silences). Each row is

as exported: { seq, at, tenantId, agentId, kind, spanId, parentId?, status, verdict, ms?, attrs }.

A model.call row's attrs carry model, session (the conversation that made the call: main, an Agents API

session, or a subagent's sub_…) and the answer's input, output, cacheRead and cacheWrite tokens, and

reasoning (the part of output spent reasoning) when the provider reported any — absent, not 0, when it

reported none, so a missing key reads as 0. Here output includes reasoning: a call's reasoning share is

reasoning / output;

on the pi085 engine also prefix (src/runtime/request-prefix.ts): the sha256 of the system prompt

and of the tool list, the request's message count and characters, how many leading messages (and their

characters) are the previous request's byte for byte, and broken — system, tools, message, or

null when the previous request is all a prefix of this one. No content is recorded.

A rebuild.decision row is a context rebuild decided (src/runtime/rebuild.ts), recorded and, unless it says

shadow: false, not acted on:

trigger cold-wake (each inbound wake of a pi085 agent's main conversation: whether the deployment's rules,

REBUILD_POLICY, predict the cache cold, predictedCold, and would rebuild, wouldRebuild — cold and over the

size threshold — with reason and the facts: gap, context tokens, model, the previous wake's first-call hit rate;

expectedCold for a first call or one after a reset), window (a pi085 context at 80% of its window, an answer

that is a context overflow, or a pd compaction that ran) or operator (a manual pd compaction), with strategy.

Its rebuild.outcome row (parentId the decision's spanId, spanId the model job) is the next call measured:

input, cacheRead, hitRate, and status agree, false-cold, false-warm (predictedCold against the

measurement), expected-cold (cold by construction, out of the rate) or unmeasured (an error, or no input).

One cold-wake rule is acted on: a wake more than idleMinutes (50; 25 for gpt-5.6-luna, whose cached prefix OpenAI keeps 30 minutes after its last use, less a

5-minute margin because this idle clock is not the provider's) after the conversation's

last answered call, when that call's input C is at least 2 × (fixed + 8,000) — fixed the agent's own system prompt

and tools in that call's request, estimated at 4 characters a token, and 8,000 the briefing's budget, so the rebuilt

request is at most half of C — is rebuilt before it is answered — a new transcript of

the same conversation whose first message is a briefing, the wake after it (a wake whose push is never delivered

leaves the briefing for the next post: it is never sent alone). The rule is per model, in

REBUILD_POLICY's idle section: { "default": {…}, "byModel": { "<model id, or a part of it>": {…} } } (an exact

id first, else the longest key the id contains; the defaults name deepseek, 50 minutes, and gpt-5.6-luna, 25, which

a moved default leaves at 25); "minContextTokens" sets an extra floor C must exceed (none by default);

"idleMinutes": null turns one model's off, "idle": false all of them. A partial policy is laid

over the default ({"idle": false} alone is the kill switch), and a policy that is refused leaves the default shadow

rules with idle rebuilds off. Its decision is that wake's

only cold-wake row: status ran, shadow: false, rule idle, idleEntry (the entry applied: default or

byModel:<key>), with the gap, the context before, fixedTokens and idleThresholdTokens (the C it needed), the briefing's tokens, blocks and budget, conversation and to

(the transcripts ended and started) and logicalConversation, and the prediction it can no longer be measured against

(predictedCold, shadowReason, and the previous measured wake's prevWakeHitRate and prevWakeGapMs); a wake the

rule passes by keeps its shadow row, whose idleRebuild and idleEntry say why (gap-under-idle,

savings-below-half, fixed-unknown, context-below-floor, needs-reset, idle-off, …), with fixedTokens and

idleThresholdTokens. The rebuilt transcript's first run, once it has ended, writes one rebuild.behaviour row

(parentId the decision), and so does, for comparison, the run of every wake past the idle time that was not rebuilt

and of a tenth of the others (rebuilt, group: rebuilt, idle-not-rebuilt, sample; idleReason), with

inboxReads counted apart from the rest: firstSendMs (the wake to the first message sent), toolCallsBeforeSend,

recapCallsBeforeSend (memory, state and message-history reads), firstAction (recap, send, act),

repeatedCalls and repeatedBeforeSend (calls with the same tool and arguments as one that succeeded among the

replaced conversation's last 12), toolCalls, modelCalls, runMs and endedWith. Numbers and flags only.

A turn.timing row is one turn of a pi085 conversation (src/runtime/turn-timing.ts), written when it ends: from a

message accepted by the agent to the reply it can read, across every pause for the caller's functions. spanId is

tt_<session>@<inboundAt>, ms the turn's wall time, status pi's last run outcome (completed, failed, aborted),

cancelled (a cancel that aborted a run or dropped waiting calls; one with neither writes no row), or abandoned

(verdict failed: the caller's new input through the Agents API arrived while the turn waited on a call the caller

never answered; the turn ends at that input, replyAt, and the input opens a new turn — a background completion, a

push or a lease warning arriving then joins the turn instead). Its attrs hold the points — inboundAt (the message entered the agent), saidAt (durable in the

transcript), turnStartAt (the first step that drove it), firstJobAt (the first model job written), lastAnswerAt

(the last answer delivered), replyAt (the step that saw the turn end returned) — and the split, whose first five

add up to ms when every job was answered: inboundToStartMs, startToFirstJobMs, modelMs (each job, written to

answered, as model.call measures it), betweenJobsMs (each answer to the next job), lastAnswerToReplyMs; then,

inside betweenJobsMs, toolMs (tools run by the agent, summed per call) and clientWaitMs (each pause, from the

step that paused to the results that completed it), and prepMs (inboundAt to saidAt), with the counts

modelJobs, tools, clientPauses and messages (a steer joins the turn in flight), and the turn's first model

call's firstCallPromptTokens and firstCallCachedTokens (how much of its prompt the provider read from cache:

DeepSeek's prompt_cache_hit_tokens, OpenAI's cached_tokens; null while that call has no answer), which tell a

first reply slowed by a cold cache from one slowed by the model. pd agents write none.

The answer adds from, to (ISO), cursor, nextCursor and scanned: { local, objects }. Nothing

is written.

Redaction

Treat every export as sensitive. It is an agent's whole conversation and trace, written by a model

that saw tool results, files and messages from people; redaction narrows what a credential in it can

reach and does not make the export safe to publish. What is caught is exactly the list below; anything

else, a credential in a shape not listed or split across two strings, is not guaranteed to be caught.

Both exports are walked whole — every string at any depth, and every key — first inside the agent's

own object, which alone can open its sealed values and never returns them, and once more by the route

(cf/src/eval-read.ts redactCredentials). Each replacement is <redacted:KIND> and is counted in

redactions at the top level. Caught:

  • By tool. A call to or result of a kept-secret tool — any tool named secret_* under any alias

(<alias>__secret_put, <alias>__secret_get, …; state.secret_get in a trace row or approval) —

loses every string in its arguments, result and approval request but the secret's name

(<redacted:kept-secret>), whatever the shape: an object, JSON in a string, or text.

  • By exact value. Every value the agent's object holds sealed — the secrets it kept, its mounts'

sealed credentials, its owner's and its hooks' secrets — the Worker environment value any of its

mounts names (env:NAME), and the operator's credential any of its mounts names (operator:exa,

operator:run9, resolved as the plugin's call resolves it; each string of a JSON one too), of 8

characters or more: each appearance, anywhere in a value or a key (<redacted:agent-secret>, one

per appearance), spelled as it is or JSON-escaped (once or twice), \uXXXX-escaped, HTML-escaped,

URL-encoded (encodeURIComponent, strict with !'()* escaped, form-encoded with + for a space,

encodeURI, and the first three encoded again), base64 or base64url (whole, or inside a longer run

at any alignment) or hex; a value of 16 characters or more, and its hex, in any letter case. And a

run that decodes to text holding one is replaced whole: a %xx-encoded run (up to three rounds of

decoding, + read both ways; only its &/?/=-separated parts that hold one, when some do) and

a base64 or base64url run of 24 to 65,536 characters, decoded from each of its first four characters.

If a sealed value or an operator credential a mount names cannot be read, the export is 503

(unavailable), not sent without this.

  • By key. The string values under a key named like a credential — containing token, secret,

password/passwd, passphrase, authorization, api_key/api-key/apikey, credential,

private_key or cookie, any case — replaced whole, and every string under such a key when its

value is an object or array.

  • By shape, whole strings: what the console refuses to send (cf/src/secret-shape.ts: API keys,

Raft agent credentials, GitHub tokens, AWS access keys, private keys, Slack tokens, URLs with a

password, labelled keys, our own service and provider tokens).

  • By shape, the credential part only: JWTs (three base64url segments, the first a JSON object);

Authorization:/Proxy-Authorization: values; Bearer <token> with a digit in it; `Basic <base64

of user:pass>; Set-Cookie:/Cookie: values; the values of X-Amz-Signature, X-Amz-Credential`,

X-Amz-Security-Token, X-Goog-Signature, X-Goog-Credential, Signature, sig, token,

access_token, refresh_token, id_token, client_secret, password, passwd, api_key and

apikey parameters; env lines *_TOKEN=, *_KEY=, *_SECRET=, *_PASSWORD=, *_PAT=,

*_CREDENTIAL(S)=, PASSWORD= (no spaces round =, not a $REFERENCE); Google AIza… keys;

Stripe sk_/rk_ live_/test_ keys; <prefix>_live_… keys; ghp_/gho_/ghs_/ghu_/ghr_

tokens of 20 characters or more and github_pat_…; sk_agent_… of 8 or more; sk-… of 20 or more.

  • Inside an encoding: JSON in a string is parsed and walked (and written back when anything in it

was replaced); %xx-encoded text holding any of the shapes, decoded up to three times, is kept

decoded; a base64 run of 24 to 65,536 characters whose decoded text holds any of the shapes is

replaced whole.

A subtree nested more than 100 deep is replaced whole (<redacted:too-deep>). A credential sealed

on a mount, and a mount's config, are not in a transcript or a trace row to begin with; the exact-value

scrub is for when the agent repeated one.

Not caught. The exact-value scrub knows only what the agent holds when the export is read: a

secret the agent deleted, or overwrote with secret_put, is no longer known, and neither is a mount's

credential since replaced or a mount since removed. A value shorter than 8 characters is not

scrubbed at all, and one shorter than 16 only in its own letter case. A value split across two strings

or two events, reversed, spaced out, compressed (gzip or deflate under base64), or in any spelling not

listed above, is not found by value. In a kept-secret tool's call, the fields that name it and say

where and when it ran (id, name, tool, toolName, callId, toolCallId, task, taskId,

status, times, sequence numbers, mount, approver…, cf/src/eval-read.ts SECRET_TOOL_KEEP)

are walked as ordinary text, so a secret the tool put in one of them is caught only by value or shape.

The shapes are heuristics: a credential with none of the shapes or keys above — a bare opaque token

outside Authorization, Bearer without a digit, a key: value or KEY = value line, an

upper-cased key prefix (SK-PROJ-…), a JWT whose header is not JSON, a header given as a

[name, value] pair or {name, value} object, or JSON embedded in prose (result: {"token":"…"}: a

string is parsed as JSON only when it starts as JSON, so a credential key inside such text is not

read as one) — is not caught unless it is one of the agent's values.

Audit

Every transcript or trace read logs one line, evt: "eval.read", with tenant, agent, route

(transcript or trace), session (transcript) or from and to (trace), cursor, count (events

or rows answered), redactions, and credentialId; never any content.

Every seed write, explicit seal, fresh context and restart logs one line, evt: "eval.seed", with

tenant, agent, op (write, seal, fresh-context, restart), the path and sha256 where

there is one, and credentialId: the provider token's

hash, the name the operator's token listing gives it. Never the token and never a file's text. A seal

made by the first push or turn logs the same line, credentialId: null, when files were seeded.

Metering model calls

What a model call costs, where that cost is recorded, and how the record is

checked; and, for every resource the ledger counts, what it is priced at, who

paid for it, what a day of it may cost before model calls are refused, and how

long its hours are kept (the last four sections). This is the contract for the pd engine (pi-durable). pi 0.85

(pi085) still meters at commit, from its own pi_usage rows

(src/store/pi-storage.ts); the one thing it shares is the model name the

consumer stamps on an answer (below).

The contract

  1. The metering boundary is the cost event: the provider answered. A model

call is metered when its answer is delivered to the agent's object, not when

the agent reads that answer.

  1. Attribution is decided separately. A paid call whose answer is refused,

or arrives for a job we no longer have (a duplicate caused by our bug, or a

lapsed take), is recorded with a verdict marker. It is neither dropped nor

billed as tenant usage. Who pays for it is a pricing decision, not a

metering one.

  1. Reconciliation uses two independent sources: the sum of the usage of the

answers the agent consumed, and pi-durable's own pi.usage documents. A

drift between them is the invariant that is checked.

The unit: one delivery attempt

Every pd model call is an ap_model_jobs job, taken by one queue attempt and

answered by deliver (PdHost.deliver, src/runtime/durable-agent.ts): a

turn's generation, a retried attempt, an overflow's retry and a compaction's

summary alike. The only call site that spends money is the queue consumer's

provider call (OpenAiCompatibleModel.complete, called from modelQueueDeps in

cf/src/index.ts), and every one of its answers reaches deliver.

So the unit is one delivery attempt, which is one paid call. deliver

writes the answer's model.tokens rows in the same transaction that decides

what happens to the answer, so a row exists exactly when the delivery was

recorded. Each row's at is the delivery's time, not the time a later commit

read the answer.

Each attempt is named by its taker, the fresh id the consumer takes the job

under (withTake, cf/src/model-queue.ts). The taker travels with the answer

(deliverAnswer), and ap_deliveries remembers each (job, taker) it has

metered for a day, so a replayed RPC for the same attempt returns the first

verdict and meters nothing.

The row names the model that was actually called. The consumer calls the

operator's model when the binding spends the operator's account and the

deployment's model otherwise, and it stamps that model on the answer, so the

ledger, the transcript and pi.usage all name it.

pi085 shares the consumer, and meters an answer under the model its entry

names (#modelOf, src/store/pi-storage.ts), so its key moves too, in one

narrow case: whenever the model called (operatorModel ?? HARNESS_MODEL)

differs from the model the job asked for. That happens when the binding's

model is changed or the binding removed while the call was queued, or when a

legacy binding does not use the operator's secret. pi085 then meters the

answer under the model called rather than the one the job asked for. When the

two agree, which is every binding left unchanged while its calls run, nothing

changes.

Verdicts

deliverywhat it ishow it is recorded
acceptedthe job's first answer, stored on its rowmodel.tokens: tenant usage. This includes an answer to a job that was already cancelled (the turn was cancelled, the model changed, the run was aborted, faulted or orphaned): the tenant's act ended the turn, and the call was paid for it.
refusedthe job already has an answer: a second attempt for one jobmodel.tokens.unaccepted
unknown jobno row for the job id on a pd objectmodel.tokens.unaccepted, then UnknownJob as before

model.tokens.unaccepted is the verdict marker. It is a resource of its own,

with the same keys (<model>:<kind>) and unit (tokens) as model.tokens, so

that:

  • the tenant's usage endpoints (readUsage and readAgentLedger,

cf/src/usage-d1.ts) leave it out with one predicate, and the /ui/usage

tiles, which draw only the resources they list, never show it;

  • no tenant price applies to it: a price is matched by its exact resource

(priceFor), so neither a model.tokens price nor its * reaches it;

  • it still travels the same outbox and the same D1 tables, so it is in

usage_hourly beside the tenant's rows, visible to the operator as our cost.

An answer with no nonzero counter writes no row in either resource (a

given-up job's error answer, for one).

Reconciliation: the drift check

/admin/diagnose reports usageDrift for a pd object (pdUsageDrift,

src/runtime/pd-outbox.ts): per provider/model key and per counter, the sum

of the usage on the answers of consumed jobs, minus the sum of every

conversation's pi.usage models bucket. Both are read with plain SQL on the

object, on demand, and nothing is written. On a healthy object every difference

is zero.

Its cost is on demand only, and grows with the object's age: SQLite reads the

usage out of every consumed job's stored answer (json_extract, no parse in

JavaScript), and nothing prunes ap_model_jobs. Nothing runs it on a

schedule.

What it compares and what it leaves out, on purpose:

  • Unaccepted deliveries are never on a job's row, so they are in neither

side.

  • Accepted answers that were never consumed (the job was cancelled before

a commit read the answer) are tenant usage but never reach pi.usage, so

only consumed jobs are summed.

  • Tool-reported usage is in pi.usage's tools bucket and is not

compared: see below.

A nonzero difference therefore means one of the two sources has something the

other does not: an answer that reached pi.usage without a consumed job (a

commit that appended an answer to a cancelled job), a pi.usage document that

is no longer stored, an answer metered under a name pi.usage does not use, or

a writer of pi.usage that is not a delivered answer.

Tool-reported usage is not money we pay

A tool result may carry usage (appendToolResult in pi-durable's

harness/tool.js adds it to pi.usage's tools bucket). That is what the

tool said it spent: a mirror of someone else's figure, not a call we paid a

provider for. The pd engine does not meter it. pi085 still writes it as

model.tokens rows under the model unknown (#modelOf in

src/store/pi-storage.ts), as a mirror; that is left as it is.

What is known not to be metered

  • A call cut off at its deadline (MODEL_CALL_DEADLINE_MS): the provider

may have spent tokens, but no answer and no usage come back.

  • A 200 response that does not parse: the call was paid, and the consumer

has no usage to report.

  • **A deliverAnswer RPC that fails before the object commits, and is

retried**: the retry is a new attempt with a new taker and a second paid

call, and at most one of the two answers is seen.

  • An answer for a job deleted by a revert to pi085: the object is a pi085

object by then, and pi085 meters at commit, so a delivery it cannot place is

not metered there.

Prices

One credit is one US dollar. usage_prices.credits_per_unit

(cf/migrations/0005_usage.sql) is dollars per unit, and every reader shows it

as an estimated dollar amount. Nothing is charged because of it; what is

refused because of it is said in "Daily limits" below.

The first prices are seeded by cf/migrations/0016_usage_prices_seed.sql, all

effective from 2026-10-09T00:00:00Z; usage before that reads as unpriced. Later

migrations add rows with later dates (0017_luna_cache_read_price.sql). Every

row is rough, to be refined, and the migration names each one's source. A

better number is a new row with a later effective_from, never an edit of an

old one, because cost is worked out when read.

resourcekeyunit$ per unitsource
model.tokensdeepseek-flash:inputtokens0.30 / 1MDeepSeek's pricing page, cache miss, peak rate (off-peak is half)
deepseek-flash:cache_readtokens(0.006 − 0.30) / 1Mcache hit $0.006/1M, as a difference (below)
deepseek-flash:outputtokens1.20 / 1MDeepSeek, peak
openai/gpt-5.6-luna:inputtokens0.20 / 1MOpenAI's pricing page, Standard, ≤272K context (checked 2026-10-11, 0017)
openai/gpt-5.6-luna:outputtokens1.20 / 1Mthe same
openai/gpt-5.6-luna:cache_readtokens(0.02 − 0.20) / 1M from 2026-10-12T00:00Z; 0 extra beforeOpenAI, cached input $0.02/1M (cf/migrations/0017_luna_cache_read_price.sql); before it, priced as input
…:reasoning, …:cache_write, …:cache_write_1htokens0 extraalready inside output / input / cache_write
model.tokens.unacceptedthe same keystokensthe sameour cost; never in a tenant's view
object.active*ms0.0000000015625Cloudflare Durable Objects, $12.50 per 1M GB-s at 128 MB
sandbox.container*seconds0.00004an estimate: run9 publishes no prices
*execs, unreadable0in the seconds; a marker, not usage
tool.callexa.searchcalls0.007Exa's per-search rate; to verify
exa.searchfailed, ms0 extrain the call

Anything else — other tools, js.run, a model not in the table — is

unpriced: its cost reads null, and the views say what they left out rather

than counting it as free.

A subset is priced at its difference. The ledger keeps three keys that

are part of another one: reasoning of output, cache_write_1h of

cache_write, and — because the clients put the provider's whole prompt into

input (usageOf in src/model/pi-bridge.ts; prompt_tokens and

input_tokens both count cache hits) — cache_read of input. A per-row

price × quantity sum is right only if each subset row carries the difference

between its rate and its parent's, so cache_read for DeepSeek is negative:

input × miss + cache_read × (hit − miss) is `(input − cache_read) × miss +

cache_read × hit. Each subset has an explicit row, so no *` can price one at

full rate, and model.tokens has no * at all.

Where cost is shown:

  • The console's usage view (/ui/usage, cf/src/usage.ts): "≈$" amounts,

"estimated cost … at rough prices", a "$ (estimated)" column.

  • /v1/agents/{id}/usage and its provider binding: every row gains a cost

(docs/agent-surface.md). Rows there are split so no kind contains another

except input, so each row's cost is its kind's whole rate, and input's is

for its uncached part; the costs add up to the ledger's.

  • GET /admin/usage-costs?from=&to=&bucket=day|month

(cf/src/admin-usage-costs.ts), the operator's: every tenant's cost by

period and resource, model.tokens.unaccepted included, with the keys each

total leaves out unpriced. Two callers may read it, as /admin/spend-limits:

a script whose x-harness-token header equals AUTOMATION_TOKEN (with no

token configured, no script passes), and a signed-in admin (isAdmin,

ADMIN_IDENTITIES), on the admin host (ADMIN_ORIGIN,

cf/src/admin-host.ts) or the console's. Someone signed in who is not an

admin gets 404, as there; with no identity at all the console's host answers

401 and the admin host sends the browser to sign in. Read-only: any other

method is 405.

  • GET /provision/usage?from=&to=&bucket=day|month

(cf/src/provision/usage.ts), the tenant's own, for Raft's panel: the same

periods and resources for the provider token's tenant only, without

model.tokens.unaccepted (readTenantCosts, cf/src/usage-d1.ts, which

prices through the one fold readUsageCosts uses), each agent's total, and

today against the daily limit below (docs/agent-surface.md, "The tenant's

spend").

Who pays

Usage the tenant paid for with their own credential is still their usage —

it is counted, in the same resource, and every view totals it — but it is

never priced: its key starts with own: (OWN_KEY_PREFIX,

src/usage/outbox.ts), and priceFor returns 0 for such a key whatever the

table holds, so a * meant for our account cannot reach it.

  • A container on a sandbox mount whose credential is the agent's own

sealed one (secretRefKind "agent") is sandbox.container / own:sandbox;

the operator's (operator:run9) stays sandbox.

  • A tool call through a mount holding the agent's own key is

tool.call / own:<plugin>.<tool> — an Exa search on the tenant's own key

is own:exa.search and costs 0; the seeded search mount on

operator:exa is exa.search and is priced. This also renames the calls

of a GitHub mount with the person's own token (own:github.…): those were

unpriced before and are 0 now.

  • A model call has no own-credential case. Every call is a queued job made

on the operator's account (callQueuedModel); an agent bound to a

credential of its own is called on the deployment's default model with the

operator's key (takeJob, cf/src/runtime.ts). So all model.tokens are

ours to pay and are priced.

Daily limits

Limits per UTC day, in US dollars at the prices above (cf/src/spend-limit.ts,

cf/migrations/0018_spend_limits.sql, cf/migrations/0021_onboarding_budget_and_plans.sql,

cf/migrations/0027_tenant_daily_budgets.sql). **Raft is the source of each

server's two budgets**; the figures below are fallbacks for a server Raft has

sent none for, and the admin's overrides are backstops that can only lower:

limitfallbackset bycounts
per tenant—Raft's dailyBudgetCents; else the plan's fallback below; an admin's override per tenant (/admin/spend-limits) can only lower itthe tenant's own agents' priced usage today, model.tokens.unaccepted left out (it is ours, not theirs)
… no budget, no plan ever said$5DAILY_LIMIT_USDas above
… no budget, free plan$3FREE_DAILY_LIMIT_USDas above
… no budget, pro plan$10PRO_DAILY_LIMIT_USDas above
platform$2000PLATFORM_DAILY_LIMIT_USDevery tenant's own agents' priced usage today, model.tokens.unaccepted and exempt tenants included
onboarding$30ONBOARDING_DAILY_LIMIT_USD; an admin's override (/admin/spend-limits, pool: "onboarding") replaces itthe priced usage today of every agent created with budget: "onboarding", in any tenant, model.tokens.unaccepted included
a tenant's onboarding share$1Raft's onboardingDailyBudgetCents; else ONBOARDING_TENANT_DAILY_LIMIT_USD; an admin's override for the tenant (pool: "onboarding", tenantId) can only lower itthe priced usage today of that tenant's onboarding agents, model.tokens.unaccepted left out

Which limit applies to a tenant. The dailyBudgetCents Raft last sent for

its server (PUT /provision/servers/{raftServerId}/plan, or a create's

POST /provision/agents; docs/agent-surface.md, kept in tenant_plans), divided

by 100; while Raft has sent none, the default of the plan Raft last said:

free $3, pro $10; else, for a tenant Raft has never given a plan, $5 — the

limit every tenant had before plans. Then an admin's override for the tenant,

when there is one, can only lower it: the limit is the smaller of the two,

and an override of null ("no limit") or above the figure changes nothing.

The tenant's onboarding share is worked out the same way from

onboardingDailyBudgetCents and ONBOARDING_TENANT_DAILY_LIMIT_USD. The

platform limit and the onboarding pool's limit are asked first and refuse

whatever Raft sent. Antiproton never asks Raft. Every surface that shows a limit

says where its figure came from (raft, plan/default, or override).

The onboarding pool is separate from both others. An agent created with

budget: "onboarding" (the hosted onboarding agent; provisioned_agents.budget,

fixed at creation) is paid by the platform: its spend is in neither its tenant's

figure nor the platform total, and its calls are judged by the onboarding limits

alone — the pool's first, then its tenant's share — not refused because its

tenant or the platform is out of money, and unable to spend the platform's budget

either way. The share is what keeps one server's users, talking on with their

onboarding agent, from spending the whole pool and stopping onboarding on every

other server: at $1 one server takes at most a thirtieth of it. Every other agent (no

provisioned row, or budget absent) is its tenant's. There is no ceiling over

both pools: the most the operator pays in a day is the platform limit plus the

onboarding limit (plus the overshoot below, and exempt tenants). An onboarding

agent in an exempt tenant is not refused either; its spend still counts toward

the onboarding total.

Each var set to off is no limit; a value that is not a number at or above 0

is logged (spend_limit.bad_config) and read as the default, so a typo does not

lift a cap. No deployment sets any of them (cf/wrangler.jsonc has none, and

production has no such secret), so the defaults above are the live figures. A tenant in DAILY_LIMIT_EXEMPT_TENANTS (comma-separated; unset is

bench) is refused by neither limit but still counts toward the platform total.

No tenant is exempt by being the operator's: the code has no such marker (the

original demo tenant also holds invited people), so an operator's tenant is

exempted by naming it in the var.

What is counted is what the ledger prices, so what costs us money: model

tokens on the operator's provider accounts, object time, operator-paid

containers, and operator-paid tool calls (Exa). A key paid with the tenant's own

credential (own:, "Who pays" above) is priced 0 and counts nothing toward

either limit; an unpriced key counts nothing, as in every view. So spend on an

unpriced key — a model with no price row, which an admin's model choice can

name — is not limited at all; each unpriced key a reading meets is logged as

spend_limit.unpriced, once per key per isolate per UTC day, so a missing price

row is visible.

Where it is checked. In the queue consumer (runModelCall,

cf/src/model-queue.ts), after the job is taken and before the provider is

called. Every model call of both engines — a main conversation's turn, a

sub-session's or background job's, a retry, a compaction's summary — is a queued

job answered there ("The unit" above), so this is the one place. A given-up job

(the dead letter queue) calls no provider and is not asked.

A refused call is answered, not thrown: the job's answer is a failed turn

whose errorMessage is the fixed `usage limit reached (out of budget); see the

turn's error and whose providerError` — what the transcript, the console and the

Agents API show (failureText) — is:

  • Daily usage limit reached ($X of $Y); resets at 00:00 UTC., or
  • Platform daily usage limit reached; resets at 00:00 UTC. (asked first: when

it is reached no call of a tenant's own agents is made), or

  • Onboarding daily usage limit reached; resets at 00:00 UTC., for an

onboarding agent when the pool is spent (asked first), or

  • This server's onboarding daily usage limit reached ($X of $Y); resets at 00:00 UTC.,

when its tenant's share is; an onboarding agent is asked nothing else.

An agent Raft runs tells Raft the same sentence: the failed call's model.call

trace row carries it (attrs.limit, modelCallRow in src/trace/seams.ts), so

the turn's BridgeFatal has the errorClass usage_limit_reached: <sentence>

(Raft's activity log shows it, up to 120 characters) and the agent's error

status has the sentence as its detail (Raft's dot shows it). Any other failed

call is model_call_failed with no detail, as before. The model never reads the

sentence: both engines leave a failed turn out of the next request.

So the queue acks the message and does not retry it; neither engine's harness

retries it (the fixed text matches none of pi-ai's retryable patterns, and "out of

budget" is in its non-retryable ones); and neither pd's compaction nor pi085's

overflow backstop reads it as an overflow, so no rebuild runs and needs_reset

is not set. The run that asked ends there with that error. The answer carries no

usage, so nothing is metered for it. At 00:00 UTC the next reading is of a new

day and calls are made again; nobody acts.

How fresh the reading is. The consumer's isolate keeps each tenant's last

reading — one D1 round trip of five parallel reads: today's hours of every

tenant with each row's agent's budget, the prices, the tenant's override, the

tenant's plan, the onboarding overrides (the pool's and the tenant's share) — for 30 s (SPEND_CACHE_MS), and for

5 s once a total that judges the calling agent is within 80 % of its limit

(SPEND_CACHE_NEAR_MS); a reading from an earlier UTC day is never used. An

admin's override, or a plan Raft sends, reaches a running isolate within those

30 s. Which pool an agent is in is read once per agent per isolate (it never

changes), beside the first reading. The ledger itself lags: an object sends its

usage at the end of the alarm pass that a delivered answer wakes, so a call's

tokens are usually in D1 within seconds of the answer.

The overshoot is bounded by that lag, not by zero. A call admitted under

the limit is made and paid for even if it crosses it; so is a call admitted on a

reading that does not yet show the crossing. So past the limit a tenant can spend,

per agent, the call in flight when it was crossed, plus any call that agent

starts within the reading's age (at most 5 s near the limit) and the ledger's

flush lag after it. An agent's calls follow one another (each waits for the last

answer and its tools), so that is one call per agent in the usual case, more only

when a flush is slow. Time the tenant keeps using without a model call is not

refused: a running container is not stopped and keeps counting, and so does the

object's own time; a new operator-paid container is not refused either, though no

new tool call reaches one without a model call first. Exempt tenants are never

refused, so the platform total can pass its limit by what they spend.

A spend read that fails admits the call (so does a failed read of the

agent's pool) and logs spend_limit.read_failed:

availability is worth more than a cap of a few dollars, and the ledger is

unchanged, so the next read that works refuses as it should. A failure is not

kept, so the next call reads again.

Overrides, for an admin (isAdmin, as /admin/models; on the admin host

too):

GET /admin/spend-limits → { defaults, overrides, events, pools, poolEvents, plans }

PUT /admin/spend-limits { tenantId, limitUsd } → the row; only lowers the tenant's limit (null: lowers nothing)

DELETE /admin/spend-limits { tenantId } → 204, or 404 when there was none

PUT /admin/spend-limits { pool: "onboarding", limitUsd } → the pool's row; limitUsd null: no onboarding limit

DELETE /admin/spend-limits { pool: "onboarding" } → 204, or 404 when there was none

PUT /admin/spend-limits { pool: "onboarding", tenantId, limitUsd } → that tenant's share; only lowers it (null: lowers nothing)

DELETE /admin/spend-limits { pool: "onboarding", tenantId } → 204, or 404 when there was none

The body's keys name what is set: tenantId alone a tenant's own limit, pool

alone the pool's, both the tenant's share of the pool. Every set and clear is kept in spend_limit_events (a tenant's) or

spend_pool_limit_events (a pool's) with who, when, and the value it replaced.

A tenant's or share's override only ever lowers the figure Raft (or the fallback)

gives, so limitUsd null there lowers nothing; a tenant is always refused at the

platform limit. plans is each tenant's plan and budgets

(dailyBudgetCents, onboardingDailyBudgetCents; null: never sent) as Raft last said them, read only. The admin host's spend-limits

page shows each tenant against its plan's limit with its onboarding agents' spend

against its share beside it, and the onboarding pool against its limit with a form

to set or clear it or a tenant's share; /admin/usage-costs gives onboarding.total and each tenant's onboarding

part (already inside its figures), and its page has an "onboarding pool" line.

Changing the platform and tenant defaults. They are code defaults, so a

deploy changes them; nothing stored holds the old $30 / $5 (no row in

spend_limits or spend_limit_events in production, read 2026-10-14). An

admin's per-tenant override, where there is one, is left as it is, and now only

lowers. A tenant's limit stays $5 until Raft sends its plan or budget.

What a tenant is shown. GET /provision/usage gives a Raft tenant its

today: its spend, its limit (null when it has none or is exempt) and where it came from (limitSource), its plan,

its onboarding agents' spend against its onboarding share (and whether they

would be refused now, by the share or by the pool being spent), whether it is exempt, whether the platform limit is reached, and whether a

call of its own agents would be refused now — standing in cf/src/spend-limit.ts, over a fresh readDaySpend

and the same judge the consumer applies, so the two cannot disagree on the rule,

only on the reading's age: the answer is kept 15 s per isolate (USAGE_CACHE_MS,

never past its UTC day; windows of at most 62 days, at most 256 answers), the consumer's reading up to 30 s (above), so they can

differ for under a minute. The platform's total and limit are never shown to a

tenant, only the boolean.

Retention

A daily Cron Trigger (17 3 *, triggers.crons in cf/wrangler.jsonc

and the preview's) runs the Worker's scheduled handler, which folds whole

days of usage_hourly older than KEEP_HOURLY_DAYS (35) into usage_daily

and deletes the hours it folded, in one batch per day (retainUsage and

foldUsage, cf/src/usage-d1.ts). A second run finds nothing to move. Every

reader sums both tables, so a total — quantity or cost — does not change when

a day is folded. The hours are kept for 35 days rather than fewer because the

views promise hourly buckets that far back: a folded day would sit in an

hourly bucket at 00:00Z as if it had happened at midnight.

The same trigger then writes the previous day's friction report (frictionDaily, cf/src/friction-scan.ts;

docs/runbook.md, "Where agents get stuck"). That step cannot throw into the handler, so a retention failure is still

what marks the invocation failed, and a friction failure never does.

Agent Experience (AX): how an official app talks to an agent

Every official app an agent uses — Raft is the most complex one, not the only one — should

expose itself in two layers, and the layer an agent is taught is designed for a reader who

thinks in turns, can be wrong about the world, and costs a model call every time it stops.

This document states the design so each app does not rediscover it.

1. Two clients, one foundation

Low level (programs, bots)AX level (agents)
PremiseNone declared; the service never checks freshnessDeclares what the model has seen; a changed world interrupts
ReturnsTyped data; an error is an errorModel-readable text, plus interruptions (§2); expected conditions never throw
StateStateless; the caller manages anything it needsRequires a state store (cursor, premise, continuations), because two steps of one agent may run in two processes
AcknowledgementThe caller decidesOnly after the model has seen what is acknowledged
  • The low level stays. A bot posting a feed item every hour has no premise to protect, and

must not be interrupted for one. It is a thin, typed, stable wrapper over the service's

routes, with one calling convention (a named object per route).

  • The AX level is built only on the low level. No private paths: anything a bot can do an

agent can do, and the difference is only experience.

  • One surface for all agents. An app's own agents and external agents learn the same text

and the same interruption shape. For Raft that means the AX client and the CLI print the

same lines; parity is then true by construction, not by alignment.

  • Agents are taught only the AX level. A model should learn how to choose after an

interruption, not how a route orders its parameters.

2. Interruptions

An interruption says: the operation did not happen, here is why, here is what changed, and

here is how to continue or give up. It protects the premise of a decision, not the

operation — so whether it can happen depends on whether the caller declared a premise.

{ state: "interrupted",
  reason: "stale_premise" | "needs_approval" | "needs_permission",
  context: "<for the model: what happened, what arrived>",
  resume: { token, expiresAt },   // continue the stored call, with the new premise
  cancel: { token } | null,       // give up, and release what was taken
  choices: "<one line: resume, cancel, or do something else>" }

The shape is the same whichever system raised it — the app (new messages arrived, a

permission is missing) or the agent runtime (a mount policy wants a person to approve). The

model learns it once.

The operating-system analogy holds and is worth keeping in mind:

  • A stale premise is futex_wait(addr, expected) / compare-and-swap: the caller passes what

it saw, the service compares atomically, and a change returns "try again". A caller that

does not care passes nothing and is never refused.

  • Needs approval is a system call interrupted by a signal: the caller declares its restart

policy (SA_RESTART for programs, the interruption handed back for agents), the service

keeps the continuation (restart_block), and the interruption is delivered only where

control returns to whoever decides — for an agent, a tool result.

  • Where it breaks: handling EINTR is nearly free; handling an interruption costs a model turn.

So interruptions must be rare and complete — carrying enough context (like siginfo) to

decide in one turn — and cancelling must release what was taken, not only drop a token.

3. Who does what

  • The service decides the condition: only it knows the newest message, who may do what,

and what a policy requires.

  • The SDK lets the caller choose whether to declare a premise, and hands interruptions back

as data: continuations are serialisable, it never retries on the model's behalf, and it

holds no closures a later process would need.

  • The agent runtime (antiproton) is where thinking is forced:
  • One place. Every tool call passes the gateway, whether the model made it or code the

agent wrote. A plugin only reports that a call was interrupted; the gateway handles it,

its own policy holds included.

  • The premise is recorded with the result. When an interruption is shown to the model,

what the model has now seen and the continuation are recorded in the same transaction as

the tool result, so a resume can only carry what the model actually saw.

  • Code cannot skip it. Inside agent-written code the interruption is neither a return

value (it could be ignored) nor an exception (it could be caught): the executor ends the

run, and the model receives the whole account — which calls completed (they happened and

are not undone), which was interrupted, and that the rest did not run.

  • Resume and cancel are the model's. They take a token, never a restated call, and are not

callable from code, so a decision cannot be taken programmatically.

  • Resolution is a wake. A person approving or clicking a card arrives as an inbound event

carrying the token.

4. Where antiproton stands

Recorded so the design is not read as a description of today:

  • Inside run_js, a call that comes back held (pending) ends the program as paused: the model

gets the outputs so far, the call count and the held operation, and the rest does not run.

There is no resume or cancel token for a hold yet: approval runs the held call alone, and the

model continues with a new program.

  • A plugin can put its SDK in the box (Plugin.bridge, #1092): a program imports it as

await import("sdk:<alias>") (or sdk:<plugin> where that names one mount), and the SDK's

fetch makes each request one host call of <alias>.request through the gateway — the same

policy, approval, operation and trace row, host-call budget and pause on a hold as any tool call.

No credential or network is in the box, which is the defence: only content-type and accept

cross from it, no header that carries a credential comes back, and the mount's credential (each

field of a stored session) and the tokens send names are replaced in the answer, or in the

message of a send that throws, where they appear (values of 8 or more characters; a JSON

answer is parsed, redacted string by string and written out again, so its bytes may differ).

  • The core first refuses a path that names a host, carries a backslash, whitespace or a control

character, or whose path part carries ;, a percent-escape or anything outside printable

ASCII, or has an empty, . or .. segment; a path past 2048 characters; a body past 1 MiB

of UTF-8 (CJK text is three bytes a character, so about a third as many characters); and a

header value with CR or LF. It drops a GET's or HEAD's body.

  • The plugin's route table then classifies the request, on the path its normalize answers

(without the query or a trailing slash, case-insensitively) and the body as sent. A row with a

body check that cannot judge the body — not sent as JSON (application/json, …+json), or

not a JSON object — refuses it if it is a refuse row, and otherwise holds it as an unlisted

write.

  • A listed route is decided under its policy key and the bridge tool's own entry, the stricter

of the two (a key only tightens, Plugin.policyKey); an unlisted read follows the mount's read

policy; an unlisted write is decided under the plugin's unlistedWriteKey, at least approval

unless the mount itself allows it; a refuse row ends the call rejected, approved or not.

confirm adds a person and never lifts a refusal.

  • A held request ends the program as above, even when the SDK's caller catches the throw, and

the model sends a new program after the approval; there is no resume, which waits on #596.

Only the dynamic worker executor loads these modules; QuickJS refuses the import

(bridge_unavailable).

  • A program can stop itself and ask: const answer = await pause(question, data, expected?).

That pause is resumable — the result is yielded, with a token, and the model answers with the

one static resume tool (or cancels); the program continues from that line. expected

(choices, yes/no, text, or a small JSON-schema subset) is checked on the server, and an answer

that does not fit is refused with the token still valid. The continuation lives only in the agent object's memory,

for at most RUN_JS_RESUME_MS (a minute by default; the object keeps itself awake meanwhile),

so it is not yet the serialisable continuation §3 asks for: once it expires or the object

restarts, resume says so and the model sends a new program, as it did before.

  • A Raft freshness hold returns the newer messages and asks the model to send again with the

same key and content; the model restates the call instead of resuming it.

  • A plugin's database writes and the recorded tool result are two transactions (#596); the

"recorded with the result" rule depends on fixing that first.

  • Raft's SDK client mixes both layers today (it tracks a premise for every caller).
source: botiverse/antiproton@d0a0e0c8
tree: 81eae7c0
built: 2026-10-28T01:41:22Z
To independently verify this build against the upstream repository: git rev-parse d0a0e0c8^{tree} (expected: 81eae7c00c5581adfd6c50718cd4621e04724798)

Write the way a sharp colleague texts. Your owner reads you on a phone, between other work.

Put the answer first. The first sentence is the answer, or the one thing they need to do. Everything after it is optional.

Cover one or two things per message. If there is more, offer it, saying how much only when a tool gave you the count: "3 more, all minor. Want them?" Without one, give no number.

Write plain sentences. Don't use headings, bold labels, tables or sign-offs, and don't open with "Here's a summary" or "Great question". Use a short list only when they ask for several things, and keep it to three lines or fewer.

Don't tell them what they already know: their own messages, what they just asked you, or how you found something out.

Point to messages; don't paste them. Quote a few words at most.

Name people by @name and say what they want: "@cy needs your OK on the launch date".

When you could not see something, say so in a few words.

A short question gets a short answer. "You there?" gets "Here. Nothing's waiting on you right now."

You also message them unasked, apart from this chat, as their contact settings say. When they ask what you will tell them about, or when, give them what their settings say, never a guess.
